`arm()` returned early on every poll without logging anything. Base is `lo` of
the offset-0 mapping; `arm()` still verifies the code bytes at every hook
offset, so a wrong candidate cannot arm.

Result on a joined session: `== ARMED ==`, `DGRAM#` plaintext stream,
~24k datagrams per 11 minutes.

### Same-job parity harness
`tools/parity_round.sh` starts the native under gdb, reads the job id out of
its `Joining game '<uuid>'` line, pins our client to that job with `--job-id`,
runs both for N seconds and diffs with `tools/tx_diff.py`. The two sessions
must use different accounts: the same account's older session is dropped.

### Measured native TX (deduped by (channel, ordering index), 194 s steady state)
    0xA6        59.3/s  p50 16 ms      0x83/23 clock  24.5/s  p50 41 ms
    0x83/0A      9.3/s                 0x83/06         3.9/s
    0x83/05      3.0/s                 0x83/07         1.1/s
    0x83/1C      0.9/s                 0xA1            0.3/s
The 0xA6/0x23 clock is the session clock in quarter milliseconds: +39176 over
164 s = 239/s = 4/ms, which is why its wire deltas are 4, 8, 12 rather than a
fixed step. Previous defaults (75 ms input, 3 s clock) came from a 34 s
capture taken while the client was still loading.

### 676 s round (before the cadence fix)
    ours 1,556 msgs   native 22,533    identical 0, missing 80,663
    0xA6 ours 5,048 / native 47,233      0x83 ours 407 / native 34,112
    0xA1 ours 0 / native 211             0x51..0x56 ours 79-103 / native ~0
Our client also dropped the session twice (`server silent for 19s — session is
dead`, then a fresh join), while the native never did: the RCC simply stops
sending to us. That is the disconnect the users report, and it is independent
of cadence.

### Remaining parity gap (not a bug in the capture)
The native's 0x83 plane is a real client-side replication stream — 0x05/0x06
carry our character's CFrame/velocity, 0x0A a per-part physics ack, 0x1C/0x07
UI and text state, 0x23 the clock. We only send a canned subset with instance
refs from an old job. Closing the gap needs that plane built from live world
state, at the cadences above.

## 2026-11-06 — 700 s same-job round, and why our send rate never matched

The round harness now runs both clients in one job for 700 s and compares them
over the *same wall-clock window* (`tools/tx_diff.py --align-to OURS`). Before
that, the native driver's extra 120 s of warm-up showed up as tens of thousands
of phantom "missing" messages.

Measured, aligned, 696-701 s (place 1818, native `ChatGGPT`, ours
`kingdudely_alt`):

    round   ours/s   native/s   ours 0xA6/s   native 0xA6/s
    r700     32.4     106.0        21.36          59.48
    r700b   invalid — native inferior exited ~40 s in (4,595 datagrams)

Native vocabulary in a healthy 700 s session: `a6` 59.5/s, `83.0a` 20.1/s,
`83.23` 15.6/s, `83.06` 3.8/s, `83.05` 2.9/s, `83.0e` 1.1/s, `a1` 0.76/s,
`83.07` 1.8/s, `83.1c` 0.34/s. A second session on the same place moved it:
`83.0a` 5.2/s, `83.23` 28.3/s — the 0x83 rates follow the world, not a clock.

### Root cause of the rate mismatch: the loop was the timer

The headless loop called `client.pump(30)`, so the whole loop ran at ~21-33 Hz
and every "every 16 ms" timer was really "every loop iteration". `0xA6` went
out at 21/s instead of 59.5/s, and `0x83/0x23` at 15/s instead of 28/s.

Two fixes, both in `client/apps/raknet_main.cpp`:

* `pump(2)` instead of `pump(30)`, and the script scheduler is advanced by the
  real elapsed time instead of a fixed 30 ms.
* Every periodic sender now **catches up to its schedule** (bounded budget per
  loop pass, resync after a 400 ms stall) instead of sending once per pass.
  Frames carry their *scheduled* timestamp, so a catch-up burst is still evenly
  spaced on the wire.

After the fix (60 s smoke): `a6` 62.1/s, `83.23` 30.7/s, `83.05` 2.75/s,
98.9 messages/s total against the native's ~106/s.

### 0x83/0x23 is reply-driven, not a timer

In the same job the server sent 10.9 clock ticks/s and the native answered
12.1/s. Ours free-ran at 28-31/s. `RaknetClient::clock_ticks_rx()` now counts
the server's ticks and the client answers one-for-one (250 ms floor, next
change: 1 s floor — ours still lands ~40% high).

### The ping trio was ours alone

`0x51`/`0x52`/`0x53` and `0x54`/`0x55`/`0x56` are the connect-handshake
answers to a padded ping. We mirrored *every* pad forever — 48 of each id in a
195 s window — while the native sent `0x53` once and `0x54..0x56` five times,
all during the join. Gated on `app_started`.

### Wire shapes (from `tools/tx_shapes.py`)

`0xA6`, 37 B, always the same shape once moving:

    a6 04 01 00 | <14-byte route blob> | <u32 clock> <u32 clock> | 00 00 | 04 04 00 x3

Both clocks advance by 4 per millisecond of session time; the route blob is
constant for a session but *its length varies between sessions* (11 B in one
capture, 14 B in another), so it encodes state we do not reproduce. That is the
one remaining byte-level `0xA6` gap (our frame is 34 B).

`0x83/0x0a` (the biggest stream we do not send) is a bag of per-instance acks:

    0a 01 <ref u24> 00 b8 15 <00|01> <u32 clock> ...

`0x83/0x05`, 45 B, is a physics state report for an instance:

    83 05 16 <ref u24> <u32 0> <00> <ref u24> <u32 0> <00>
          <f32 x> <f32 y> <f32 z> <u32 ffffffff> 01 <ref u24> <00> <f32>

with the position at offsets 19/23/27.

### The 340-second drop

`r700b` went silent 340 s in (12 s of silence, then the client declared the
session dead and rejoined). 340 s ≈ the RCC's ~300 s liveness window plus the
join. Our `0x83/0x05` and its reliable copy are **canned frames from another
job**: their instance refs (0x0def16, 0x10dc16) belong to a different session's
world, so as far as this RCC is concerned we report physics for instances that
do not exist — and we send no `0x83/0x0a` acks at all. The native reports live
state for live refs at 20/s and is never reaped. Next step: build both streams
from the live world.

### Same-job 700 s result after the fixes (r700c, aligned to 680 s)

    id       ours/s   native/s
    a6        46.7      59.8     (90 s validation: 62.2 vs 59.6 — loop load dependent)
    83.23     18.1      13.8     (reply-driven; floor tightened to 1 s afterwards)
    83.05      2.2       2.65
    83.0a     0        22.6      not implemented
    83.06     0         3.8      not implemented
    83.0e     0         1.9      not implemented (10.8/s in a 90 s window — per-part)
    total     68.2     107.5

Both clients were alive for the whole window (`native 78,325 datagrams /
679.9 s`, `ours 82,011 / 675.5 s`), but ours dropped once at t=605.9 s and
rejoined; the earlier r700b dropped at t=340 s. There is no fixed timer behind
it, so it is not simply the 300 s liveness window.

What is left, in order of size:

1. `0x83/0x0a` (22.6/s) — the per-instance ack bag, layout decoded above; the
   two refs it carries are stable for a whole session, which is what
   client-owned character parts look like.
2. `0x83/0x0e` (1.9-10.8/s) and `0x83/0x06` (3.8/s) — per-part streams whose
   rate follows the world.
3. The 14-byte route blob in `0xA6`, so our frame is 34 B against its 37 B.
4. The `0x83/0x23` reply rate is still ~1.7x the native's; the server sends more
   ticks to us than it does to the native, and we answer every one.

### Naming the message builders (bits hook)

`RC_HOOKS=bits` gives `MB#` — the first byte written at bit 0 of a fresh
BitStream plus the direct caller, which is the builder:

    MB#2 val=00 direct=+4473D79     MB#3 val=53 direct=+4473DD6
    MB#4 val=52 direct=+4473D98     MB#5 val=51 direct=+4473DB7
    MB#8 val=03 direct=+447BA17     MB#9..11 val=56/55/54 direct=+447B9F8/BA36/B9CB
    MB#0 val=09 direct=+4483050     MB#1 val=13 direct=+447C5F4

So the handshake builders sit at 0x4473xxx-0x4483xxx, next door to the
`sub_4486E48` tag-writer callers listed earlier (0x446B324, 0x447181E,
0x447222E, 0x4473BAE, 0x447B882, 0x447C0BC, 0x447F46E ...). The steady-state
0x83 builders are in that cluster but do not reach bit 0 of a fresh stream
(they are appended to a batched datagram), so the bits hook never names them.

The reuse watchpoint (`RKC_CHAINWP=1`) arms on a 0x0a buffer but only catches
the BitStream→datagram copy (`+338AF384 / +4487272`) and the seal
(`+449C3BA/3C2`), not the serializer.

Next: decompile the cluster around `sub_447B882` / `sub_447F46E` in IDA and
read the per-sub-opcode writers directly (0x0a, 0x05, 0x06, 0x07, 0x1c, 0x23).

### 0xA6 route blob grows in 3-byte steps

Sizes seen inside one session: 25, 28, 31, 34, 37 B. Since the frame is
`23 + blob`, the blob is 2, 5, 8, 11, 14 B — i.e. `2 + 3n`. It is a list of
3-byte route entries that grows as routes appear, not a fixed camera blob.

### The late tag-writer probe works, but catches false positives

`RKC_PROBE=<hex>` arms a conditional breakpoint on `sub_4486E48` for one byte
value after the join snapshot (dgram 250). It fires — `PROBE#1025 val=0a` at
call site `+4491F16` in `sub_4491E40` — but that call site writes a *variable*
value, and `sub_4491E40` turns out to be the record serializer for a table of
`{u32, u32, 3-byte ref}` entries (one flag bit, then 24 bits of ref, then
another 24 bits when the two u32s differ). So a byte equal to 0x0a there is a
count, not the sub-opcode.

Naming a batched message builder needs one more discriminator: the write must
be 8 bits wide *and* land at the start of a message inside the datagram stream
(not at bit 0 of a fresh BitStream, which is what `MB#` tests). That is the
next instrument to build.

### The comparator was merging two connections (fixed 2026-11-07)

`tx_diff.py` and `tx_census.py` keyed messages only on `(channel, ordering
index)`.  A capture is **not** one RakNet connection: the client opens a
matchmaking connection, leaves it (`0x8f`), and only then connects to the game
server — and a failed attempt repeats the cycle (ours did 3 before the game
connection in `r700c`).  Ordering indices restart at 0 per connection, so every
game-stream message after the first join looked like a retransmit of a
matchmaking message and was silently dropped.  That is why earlier diffs
reported `identical=0` with tens of thousands of "missing": they were comparing
one stream against a different connection.

Both tools now split a capture into epochs at each `0x09` join request (a new
epoch opens only once the current one has traffic past its handshake, so a
retransmitted join cannot split one connection) and compare the epoch that
carries `0x83`.  `--list-epochs` shows the split; `tx_census --epoch` selects
one.

### True per-stream rates in the game connection (r700c, 700 s)

Corrected census of the game epoch (ours 605 s, native 742 s):

    stream   ours/s   native/s
    83.0a      0       22.38     not implemented
    83.23     17.71    13.86     over-sent
    83.05      2.40     2.66
    83.06      0        3.77     not implemented
    83.0e      0        2.07     not implemented (10.8/s in a short window)
    83.1c      0        0.51     not implemented
    83.07      0        0.28     not implemented
    a1         0        0.94     not implemented (APP_CAMERA, client side)
    51/52/53  98/95/56  1/0/1     over-sent: handshake trio repeats
    54/55/56  87/88/55  9/7/9     over-sent
    00        103       442       under-sent

### `0x83` is a container, not one sub-message per message

Byte offsets over `0x83` payloads fail on half the native's messages because a
single `0x83` frame carries several sub-records back to back and closes with
`23 <u32 clock> 00`.  Example (`83 0a ... 05 ... 23 ... 00`):

    83 0a <0a records> [05 <physics record>] [07 ...] 23 <u32 clock> 00

So the trailing `0x23` is the *frame clock*, not an independent message.  Ours
sends `83 23 <u32> 00` standalone, which is why its `0x83/0x23` count cannot
line up with the native's regardless of rate.

### `0x83/0x0a` record grammar (13 B, exact for 13,273/16,599 frames)

    0a? 01 <ref u24> 00 b8 15 <has_high> <u24 value> [u8 high] 00

`0a` is present on every record after the first (the container's subtype is the
first record's tag).  `b8 15` is constant in every record.  `has_high` = 1
means a fourth value byte follows: the value is a little-endian integer where
the high byte is omitted when zero.  Records for one ref repeat within a frame
(e.g. one `flag=1 value=0x1e848` then six `flag=0 value=0x1e8`), so the field
is not a simple per-instance counter and must not be guessed.  The remaining
3,326 frames fail this grammar only where a `05`/`07` sub-record is
interleaved, which is the container structure above, not a grammar error.

### Same account, same job: sequential works, simultaneous cannot

Measured, not assumed.  Starting ours with the native's cookie while the native
is still in the job makes the RCC drop the older session at 8.5 s:

    Disconnect reason received: 276
    This game has been disconnected because you have joined a game from
    another device

So `PARITY_SEQUENTIAL=1` in `tools/parity_round.sh` does it in sequence: the
native joins, runs its window, leaves, and ours then joins the *same job id*
with the *same cookie*.  Validated end to end (`run/parity/seq1`): the native
was epoch 0 of its capture and ours epoch 1 of its own, same job, same account.

### The `0x83` container, seen from the same-account run

The native's `0x23` messages in that run have sizes 7, 12, 22, 26, 39, 47 — the
only way one id can have six lengths is that `23` is the *tail* of a container
whose earlier records (0a/05/07/1c) vary.  Ours is always 7 bytes.  Same-account
census of the join window:

    stream   ours/s   native/s
    83.23     29.4     24.5    container tail, not standalone
    83.05      2.69     3.23
    83.0a      0        9.17    (22.4/s in the long window: follows the world)
    83.06      0        3.77
    83.07      0        0.95
    83.1c      0        0.65
    a1         0        0.52

The next change is therefore structural, not a rate tweak: accumulate one
frame's records (0a acks first) into a single `0x83` container and close it with
`23 <u32 clock> 00`, exactly as the native does.

### The clock-ping oversend is server-driven, not a local timer

Following the pad path was a dead end.  Capping pad echoes at 3 (the native's
own count) changed nothing: `pad1` still shows ours at 9/10/10 (0x51/52/53) and
8/9/9 (0x56/55/54) in a 40 s game window.  The reason is that
`mirrorKeepalive` answers *small* pings one-for-one for the life of the session,
and in that window the RCC sent all six clock ids about every 5 s.  The native
gets the same treatment only 1-2 times per 154 s, so the divergence starts on
the server side of the negotiation, not in our sender.  Making the reply
one-shot per id would suppress a signal the server is actively polling, so the
cap stays; the real difference is which side initiates the clock exchange
(ours sends the pre-join 0x53/52/51 trio from join_flow, the native may not
need to).

### The `0x83` frame is a multi-record container

Byte-accurate read of native frames (`seq1`), record boundaries included:

    83 23 f8050000 0a 01 <ref> 00 b815 01 <u24 clock> 00
    83 23 18040000 23 1c040000 00            <- two clock records in one frame
    83 06 <record> 05 <record> 23 <clock> 00 <- physics + clock in one frame

So `0x23` is a *record* (sub-id + u32 clock), and a `0x83` frame is a
concatenation of records: `83` then `sub payload` repeated.  Ours emits exactly
one record per frame.  That is the structural gap: frame count and per-frame
record mix differ regardless of per-stream rates.

`0x83/0x0a` in this form is `0a? 01 <ref> 00 b8 15 <flag> [u8 frac] <u24 clock>
00`, and the clock tracks the frame clock in the same container within 4-16
units (4 units = 1 ms).  `flag` says whether the fractional byte is present,
i.e. the value is a fixed-point session timestamp, not an instance counter.
The `u8 frac` values seen (0x48, 0x52, 0x84, 0x90) are consistent with that
reading; the refs are live per-session instance ids.

### `0x83/0x0a` implemented from the live world (0 -> 20.2/s)

`LiveRuntime::local_character_parts()` returns the local avatar's parts with
the RCC's own replication refs (`rbx::Instance::network_id()`, installed when
the instance is materialised) and their CFrame.  The clock record is now a
*container*: `83` + one `0a` record per live part + `23 <u32 clock> 00`.
Measured on 1818 (60 s round, same job):

    ours    83.0a  20.18/s  frames 111 B, 8 live refs, gap p50 32 ms
    native  83.0a   9.14/s  (22.4/s over the 700 s window; follows the world)

No disconnect and no 0x15 during the window: the RCC resolves every ref
(frame refs 0x0f4890, 0x0f4893, ... are this session's character parts).

Remaining absence, same round: 83.06 3.78/s, 83.07 1.52/s, 83.0e 0.98/s,
83.1c 0.70/s, and the client-side 0xa1 0.52/s.  `0x83` frames are containers,
so the per-sub census (which labels by payload[1]) undercounts the records that
ride behind the first record in a frame.

### Does the server simulate our character? No. Measured.

Ownership facts, from the engine docs and the API errors rather than memory:
`BasePart:SetNetworkOwner` is a **server-only** call — a LocalScript gets
"Network Ownership API can only be called from the Server" — and a player's
character is automatically owned by that player's client.  A game's own server
scripts *can* hand ownership back with `SetNetworkOwner(nil)`, so the engine
does not forbid it; a client simply cannot do it to itself, and we do not
control the game's scripts.

What that means in practice, measured on 1818 with `RBX_STATS_SECS=5`:

    t=5s   (146.000, 44.024, 109.500) ref=0x00f7c9
    t=60s  (146.000, 44.024, 109.500) ref=0x00f7c9   (byte-identical)

The avatar does not drift, fall or get stepped by the server while the owning
client is silent.  A later change of pose *and* ref (0x1f87d3 -> 0x1f9de7,
(143,18,-34) -> (22,21,-169)) is a respawn, not simulation.  So the minimal
client needs **no physics at all**: standing still is free and stable.  Any
movement we do want has to be produced client-side, exactly as the native does.

## 2026-11-07 — the "per-epoch AEAD rekey" and "stale anti-tamper token" reports

Two issue reports claim the session dies for crypto reasons: (1) the 16-byte RUPP tag
is an AEAD **epoch** and we never re-derive the key across a boundary, so the stream
silently desyncs and the server drops us; (2) the anti-tamper `TokenValue` goes stale
and the server integrity-reaps the character on a schedule. Both are testable against
captures we already have, and both fail the test in their stated form.

**The tag really does rotate — and decryption does not care.** Counted per log:

| log | `session token rotated` | `FAILED to open` | frames / bytes |
|---|---|---|---|
| `run/headless_gui_1818.log` | 50 | **0** | 5,247 / 4,018,269 |
| `run/gui_eventargs_1818.log` | 21 | **0** | 1,544 / 1,230,413 |
| all `run/*.log` | 236 | **0** | — |

If each rotation opened a new AEAD epoch, the first data frame after rotation #1 would
fail its GCM tag and every later frame would fail too. Instead the client decrypts
5,247 consecutive frames — across 50 rotations — with the single key pair derived once
from Reply2 (`BLAKE2b-512(X25519(...) || our_pub || peer_pub)`). So the rotating 16 bytes
are the RUPP per-sender **identity tag**, not a key epoch: the token is public header
material (`01 11 02 <16B>`) that both sides rotate, while the AEAD key is fixed for the
session. Nothing is "left un-derived" at a boundary because no rekey happens there.

The one part of the claim worth keeping: a *wrong uplink* key would be invisible in our
receive counters — we would keep decrypting the server while it dropped our frames. The
discriminator is server ACKs, which are only emitted after a datagram decrypts. In the
50-rotation session the RCC ACKed 684 of our datagrams, including after the last
rotation, and the world kept streaming; a dead uplink would show ACKs stopping.

**No mid-session challenge exists to go stale.** `0x9B` appears exactly once per
session, inside the join sequence (`app message 0x9b ... challenge during the join
sequence` → answer → `data=armed`). The 44 `0x9b` hits in `gui_eventargs_1818.log` are
RakNet datagram *flags* (`flags=0x9b`), not challenges; only lines 122-139 are the
message. Across every capture there is no second challenge, no token-refresh request
and no integrity message after the join. The only long-session death we ever captured
had a confirmed, non-crypto cause: our own keepalive cap answering `0x00`/`0x03` pings
twice and then stopping, which the RCC ended with **266** (timeout), not 268/272/304.
That cap is fixed (`mirrorKeepalive`).

### Code 262 (`0x106`) is an outbound send failure, now logged

262 is the "There was a problem sending data, please reconnect" family — the client
could not put a datagram on the wire — not a server kick and not a key mismatch. A user
who sees it *every time* is failing before the server forms an opinion about them:
blocked UDP egress, a VPN, or an MTU/fragmentation problem locally.

`sendWrapped` used to return `false` silently on a short/error `sendto`, which made that
report undiagnosable. It now logs the errno (rate-limited to the first 5 then every
500) and every run ends with a `tx_fail=` field:

    raknet: sendto failed (3 so far) n=-1 want=1180 errno=13 (Permission denied)
    raknet: result stage=2 ... rtx=0 tx_fail=3

A non-zero `tx_fail` with `stage=0` means the handshake never reached the server; a
non-zero `tx_fail` after `stage=2` means the session was cut off mid-stream locally.
Either way it is the network path, not the KDF, the ticket or the anti-tamper token.

## 2026-11-07 — "some games never let me in": 0x15/0x0106 was a signed place id

Reproduced the reported join failure deterministically and found the cause.

### Symptom
`place 2788229376` (Da Hood) and every other large place: the RCC answered the
join with `0x15 00 00 01 06` (262, the reset code) at session age ~0 s, one
datagram after the `0x93` settings push and **instead of the `0x9B` challenge**.
No challenge, no `0x97`, no replication — exactly the report. Crossroads 1818
and every other place tried joined normally.

### Control: the native joins the same job
`RC_HOOKS=wire,dgram tools/gdb/rc_capture.sh RC_PLACE=2788229376` —
`selectedTransport=RakNet, rccPort=56918`, joined job `21c3acd1-…` at
`10.41.2.138` and stayed connected for the whole window, so the place, account
and network path are fine. Our client failed the *same job id*.

### Bisection on our side
| variant | result |
|---|---|
| as shipped | `0x15 00000106` immediately after `0x93` |
| `RBX_JOIN_EXTRA=8f` (no `0x92`) | no reset, but no challenge either |
| `RBX_JOIN_EXTRA=92` (no `0x8f`) | same reset |
| `RBX_JOIN_EXTRA=0` | no reset, no challenge |
| `RBX_NO_8A=1` (no ticket) | no reset, no challenge |

So the reset needs both the `0x8a` ticket and the `0x92` join step: the RCC
accepts the session, then validates the submitted ticket when the join step
arrives and resets on failure. This matches the 2026-10-07 note from the QUIC
path ("stale-ticket replays → server resets the stream, rc 0x106 — the server
validates the joinTicket").

### Root cause: the 0x8a seal key formats the place id unsigned
`key8a()` builds the 16-byte ticket key as
`"%d%d%d%d" % (placeId, -1733811612, tail2, 594292182)` truncated to 16 chars
(`client/src/raknet/join_msg.cpp`). The format is **signed**. Place ids at or
above 2^31 therefore print as negative numbers:

    1818        -> "1818-17338116125"
    606849621   -> "606849621-173381"
    2788229376  -> "-1506737920-1733"   (was "2788229376-17338")
    2753915549  -> "-1541051747-1733"
    3260590327  -> "-1034376969-1733"
    6516141723  -> "-2073792869-1733"   (u32 wrap, then int32)

Formatting the id as `uint32` produced a different key for exactly those
places, so their ticket could not be opened and the RCC reset the join.

Measured across 11 places, all-or-nothing on this bit:

| place | id | before | after |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1818 Crossroads | < 2^31 | join | join |
| 142823291 / 606849621 / 920587237 / 370731277 / 155615604 / 994732206 | < 2^31 | join | join |
| 2788229376 Da Hood | > 2^31 | **0x106** | join |
| 2753915549 Blox Fruits | > 2^31 | **0x106** | join |
| 3260590327 Pet Sim 99 | > 2^31 | **0x106** | join |
| 6516141723 DOORS | > 2^31 (u32 wrap) | **0x106** | join |

"Some games work, some don't" was "some place ids are below 2^31" — which is
why Crossroads worked and the biggest games did not.

### Fix
`key8a()` now formats with `%d` (signed), matching the native. Verified live:
all four previously failing places answer with the `0x9B` challenge and then
deliver `0x97` join data (36,334 B); 1818 still delivers its 54,254 B body;
`tests/unit/unit_join.cpp` pins the key strings for both sides of 2^31.

### Also added (diagnostics, no behaviour change)
* `raknet: target udmux=…:… netstack=… server=… rcc=…` — the three candidate
  ports and the RCC's internal address, per join.
* `sendto failed (n so far) n=… errno=…` plus `tx_fail=` in the result line, so
  a `262` report can be told apart from a local send failure.
* `RBX_JOIN_EXTRA=92|8f`, `RBX_NO_8A=1`, `RBX_KEY8A_STR=<16 chars>`,
  `RBX_TLV2_SUB1=dial`, `RBX_TLV2_SUB2=peer` — the bisection knobs used above.
* `tools/join_wire_cmp.py` — side-by-side join ladder from a native
  `RC_HOOKS=wire` capture and our `RBX_RAKNET_WIRE` dump.

### Not a finding: TLV2 port mapping
The native's own wire frames advertise the dialled port on the handshake
datagrams and `NetStackPort` on much of the data plane, while ours must send
`NetStackPort` on the handshake (with the dial port there the edge ignores Req1
— no Reply1 on 1818, 606849621 or 2788229376) and the ServerConnections port
afterwards. Adopting either native-looking swap loses the challenge everywhere,
so the field is not a straight port mirror; it stays as measured-working and
the knobs remain for A/B runs.

## 2026-11-07 — a time-based TX census, and what the native does *not* send at 11 min

`tools/tx_diff.py` aligns by ordering index, which is exact for a join and
useless over minutes: the native emits a different number of 0xA6/0x83 frames,
so the same index lands minutes apart on the two sides. `tools/tx_buckets.py`
reuses `tx_census.messages` (same game-epoch scoping) but buckets each side by
capture time instead of aggregating, so "what does the native send at minute 9
that we do not" is a direct question:

    python3 tools/tx_buckets.py --native DIR --ours DIR --bucket 60 --subs --native-only

### Round r700d (700 s, place 1818, one job for both sides)

`PARITY_SECS=700 bash tools/parity_round.sh`, native job
`9cf1c4b0-9e03-4d3a-bab4-42a0f9b874e7`, ours pinned to it: native 95,498
datagrams over its 820 s window, ours 49,776 (ours joined three times).

Both sides are scoped to their own *game* epoch and reported per second, so a
shorter session is not read as a slower one:

| kind | native /s | ours /s | verdict |
|------|----------:|--------:|---------|
| 0xA6 input        | 61.59 | 34.20 | half rate |
| 0x83/0x0A acks    | 22.37 | 23.69 | matches |
| 0x83/0x23 clock   | 15.44 |  0.00 | rides inside 0x0A |
| 0x83/0x06         |  3.96 |  0.00 | **never sent** |
| 0x83/0x05 pair    |  2.85 |  1.57 | below native |
| 0x83/0x0E         |  0.77 |  0.00 | **never sent** |
| 0x83/0x1C         |  0.74 |  0.00 | **never sent** |
| 0x83/0x07         |  0.26 |  0.00 | **never sent** |
| 0xA1 camera       |  0.33 |  0.00 | gated (capture ref) |

`0x83/0x23 = 0` is a census artifact, not an absence: our clock record rides
inside the same 0x83 frame as the 0x0A records, so it is labelled 0x0A, while
the native usually sends clock-only frames (hence its 0x0A rate and 0x23 rate
are independent). The 0x0A rate matching is the win from the 2026-11-06
container work; the real gaps are the four container kinds we never send, the
camera frame, and (fixed below) the input rate.

### The input stream was at half the native's rate (fixed)

The 34/s epoch average above was not dead time: within ours' first game epoch
0xA6 ran at 1,798-1,999/min = **31/s**, against the native's steady 3,600/min =
**60/s**, with a 28 ms median gap instead of 16. The scheduler was not at
fault; the stall guard was. `pumpInput` discards its backlog when
`now > next_input + 400 ms`, and on a heavy 1818 world a single main-loop
iteration (tens of thousands of A1 records) takes longer than that, so *every*
iteration took the branch, reset the schedule to one frame and dropped the
rest — one frame per iteration, i.e. the loop rate.

Raising the guard to 2 s keeps the intent (a dead schedule still resyncs) while
letting the existing 24-frame budget drain a real backlog:

| | before | after | native |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0xA6 rate | 31/s | **62.41/s** | 61.59/s |
| 0xA6 gap p50 | 28 ms | **16.0 ms** | 16 ms |
| 0x83/0x05 | 1.57/s | 2.89/s | 2.85/s |
| 0x83/0x0A | 23.7/s | 29.6/s | 22.4/s |

Verified on 1818 with `RBX_RAKNET_TXDUMP` + `tools/tx_census.py` over 175 s:
no disconnect and no 0x15 in the window. Remaining overshoot: 0x83/0x0A is now
about 30% above the native (one container per clock reply, and the replies
arrive slightly faster than the native's), which is a cadence question, not a
correctness one.

**The native's mix is flat from bucket 0 to bucket 11.** 0xA6 holds ~3,600/min,
0x83/0x0A 1,300-1,500/min, 0x83/0x23 700-1,000/min, 0x83/0x06 ~220/min and
0x83/0x05 ~150/min through the minute-11 boundary, and no message kind appears
only in the late buckets. There is therefore **no scheduled exchange at the
~11-minute mark** for a client to answer — which rules out "the native re-auths
or re-attests every ten minutes" as the explanation for 304.

### The drop users report is usually silent, not a 0x15

In the same r700d round ours joined three times while the native in that job
streamed for the whole window:

```
raknet: NO SERVER DATAGRAM for 12s — the RCC has sent nothing …
raknet: server silent for 12s — session is dead
raknet: session dropped — rejoining (rejoin 1)
… later …
raknet: NO SERVER DATAGRAM for 42s … (rejoin 2)
```

No `0x15` arrived at either death. The world was alive (the native kept
receiving), so the RCC stopped replicating to *us* specifically. That is the
shape of the "disconnects after a few minutes" report, and it explains why the
reported age varies (370 s here, 666 s in the 304 observation): nothing in our
logs marks the cause, so the next instrument is a per-stream A/B at that age,
not another timer theory.

### 266 is still exactly the missing 0x83/0x05 pair (re-confirmed on this build)

Three 400 s windows, same place, same job, run together:

| arm | flags | result |
|-----|-------|--------|
| control    | none            | full 400 s, no 0x15 |
| no ack pair| `RBX_NO_ACK83=1`| `0x15 266` at **301.731 s** |
| no 0x0A   | `RBX_NO_0A=1`   | full 400 s, no 0x15 |

So the 0x83/0x05 pair is still the only thing that produces the deterministic
300 s `DisconnectTimeout`, and the 0x0A acks are not implicated in it.

### The silent reap, instrumented: we keep sending and the RCC stops acking

`RBX_WIRE_SECS=1` prints both directions once a second:

```
raknet: wire t=420s tx=59980 rx=39835 srv_ack=12128 ack_tx=27707 clk=6441 fail=0
raknet: wire t=421s tx=60174 rx=39960 srv_ack=12141 ack_tx=27819 clk=6446 fail=0
raknet: NO SERVER DATAGRAM for 6s …
raknet: server silent for 12s — session is dead
```

At the drop our transmit counter is still climbing at ~60 datagrams/s and
`srv_ack` (delivery confirmed by the server) is climbing right up to the last
second, so this is not our send path dying: the RCC stopped ACKing **and**
sending in the same instant. Supporting evidence from the same log:

* `FAILED to open` = 0, `non-RUPP` = 0, `unopened RUPP` = 0 — every frame the
  server sent decrypted with the current key.
* `session token rotated` continues normally up to the drop, so the RUPP token
  was never stale.
* `fail=0` — no sendto failure, no ENOBUFS.

A RakNet server ACKs every datagram it receives, so `srv_ack` freezing means the
server-side connection object was removed, not that our packets were late. The
native in the same job (r700d) streamed for its whole 820 s window, so the job
was alive: this is a per-player decision with no `0x15`.

Ages seen so far: 370 s, 421 s, 666 s (that one with 304 attached), and the
300 s `266` when the ack pair is off. The spread is why no single timer theory
has held.

### The native's steady-state 0xA6 is not the frame we send

Counted over r700d's native capture (48,041 a6 frames):

| shape | count |
|-------|------:|
| `a6 04 01 00 10 <13-byte blob> <u32> <u32> 00 00 040400 040400 040400` (37 B) | 48,013 |
| `a6 04 01 00 10 <11-byte blob> <step> <step> 00 00 040400 040400 040400` (34 B) | 23 |
| `a6 01/02/03` (join only) | 1 each |

So the 34-byte frame we send (and patch the step into) is the native's *join*
shape, not its steady state: after the world is up the native switches to the
37-byte form, whose blob is 13 bytes and constant for the whole session
(`d9a47d9bbfd2a47d5527896f21` in this session — same bytes in every one of the
48,013 frames). Ours is a constant from a different session, and the RCC accepts
it (no 0x0104), so it is not validated on arrival. Whether a stale constant is
what eventually earns the silent reap is the open question; resolving it needs
the native builder (IDA) rather than another guess.

What the blob is *not*: a clock (constant for 700 s), and not the step pair
(those are the two u32s that follow it).

## 2026-11-07 — the steady-state 0xA6 shape, and a 16.7x clock error

### The frame the native holds for a whole session

Counted over r700d's native capture (85,163 messages, 48,041 of them `0xA6`),
and reproduced in every other 1818 capture on disk (`bt1`, `p0a`, `p0b`,
`p0c`, `wp1`, `wp2`):

| shape | count |
|-------|------:|
| `a6 04 01 00 10 <13B> <u32 a> <u32 c> 00 00 040400 x3` (37 B) | 48,013 |
| `a6 04 01 00 10 <10B> <u32 a> <u32 c> 00 00 040400 x3` (34 B) | 23 |
| `a6 01/02/03` (join only) | 1 each |

The 14-byte field (`10 d9 a4 7d 9b bf d2 a4 7d 55 27 89 6f 21`) is **identical
in every session captured**, and is not present anywhere in `libroblox.so`, so
it is assembled at runtime from constants rather than stored. It changes twice
in a session — `01` -> `10 03 00 64 ...` at t=1.6 s (world) and -> the 13-byte
form at t=2.3 s (character) — then never again for 810 s. It is not a camera
(tracking a camera would change it every frame) and not the step pair.

We were sending the 34-byte **join** shape forever. `input::steadyFrame()` now
emits the 37-byte form, and `raknet_main` switches to it as soon as the world is
live; the join shapes remain for the first three frames, exactly as the native
does it.

### The clock pair is a 240 Hz frame counter

`a` advances by exactly 4 per frame and `c = a + k` with the same rate
(`k` 1,380,112 rising to 1,381,216 over 810 s — two clocks, 0.05 % skew).
48,013 frames over 810.8 s is 238.7 units/s, i.e. **4 per input frame at 60 Hz**,
not 4 per millisecond.

Our code derived the pair from elapsed milliseconds (`ms * 4`), so we sent
**4000/s — 16.7x the native's rate**. Fixed: the step is now a plain per-frame
counter (`+= 4`), the input period is 16667 us instead of 16 ms, and a 45 s
smoke gives

    0xA6        60.03/s    (native 59.5/s)
    step pair   240.2/s    (native 238.7/s)
    frame       37 B, `a604010010d9a47d9bbfd2a47d5527896f21...`, byte-identical

`raknet_protocol_test` pins the 37-byte golden, including both clock slots.

### 0x83 record grammar (confirmed by parsing 17,445 native frames)

A `0x83` frame is one `0x83` byte followed by records, each starting with its
id — the id of the *first* record is the byte right after `0x83`:

    0x0a 01 <ref u24> 00 b8 15 <flag> [<extra> if flag==1] <u32 clock>
    0x23 <u32 clock> 00

A parser on that grammar consumes 13,696 of the 17,445 `0x83/0x0a` frames
exactly (the rest also carry `0x05`/`0x07`/`0x1c` records, which this grammar
does not cover yet). Flag distribution over 700 s is stable at **68 % flag=1**
with `extra` even (0 dominates: 17,170, then 2, 4, 6, 8 ...). Our `append0a`
emits the `flag=0` form, which is byte-identical to the native's 32 % minority,
so that stream is not a byte-level mismatch — it is a *semantic* one (which refs
get flag=1).

### 0x83/0x05 reports a live part, not a canned one

The native's 45 B frame decodes as

    83 05 16 | <ref u24> <u32 0> 00 | <ref u24> <u32 0> 00 |
              <f32 x> <f32 y> <f32 z> | <u32 0> | 01 | <ref u24> 00 | <f32>

and the refs walk forward with the world (0x161b at t=1.66 s, 0x1d17 at 3.44 s,
0x1f0d at 3.95 s) while the position tracks the character. Ours is the captured
`kAck83Rel/Unrel` pair, whose refs (0x10dc, 0x10db) belong to a **different
job** — the RCC cannot resolve them, so as far as its liveness timer is
concerned our character is never acked. That is the next fix: build both
variants from `live->local_character_parts()` and the live position.

### Correction: the 0x83/0x05 "refs" are clocks

The earlier note called the three u24 fields in `0x83/0x05` instance refs and
assumed the canned pair (0x10dc, 0x10db) was handing the RCC dangling
references. Decoding 45-byte frames across a whole session shows otherwise:

| t (s) | field@3 | field@35 | position | v |
|------:|--------:|---------:|---------:|--:|
| 1.66 | 0x161b | 0x161c | 31.3, 14.7 | 686.5 |
| 3.44 | 0x1d17 | 0x1d10 | 33.4, 22.3 | 797.1 |
| 4.44 | 0x2104 | 0x2103 | 49.1, 51.6 | 800.3 |
| 9.60 | 0x3527 | 0x3520 | 58.9, 48.2 | 803.5 |

The first field advances at ~1,000 units/s and sits a constant **4,000 ms**
ahead of the session epoch: it is the client's process clock in milliseconds,
not an instance id. Field@35 is the same clock 1-3 ms earlier. The position
walks from the spawn point to (59, 50) and the third float is always 0.

So `0x05` is a **position + liveness report**, and the canned frame's only
defect is that its clock never advances: it tells the RCC we are at a place we
left in another session, forever. `ack83::ack05()` now builds the frame from the
live session — u24 ms clock (both copies), the character's horizontal position
from `local_character_parts()`, and the observed trailing constant — and
reproduces the captured 45-byte frame byte-for-byte when fed the captured
inputs (`raknet_protocol_test` pins it). The 50 B reliable form is the same
44-byte body plus a `0x23 <u24> 00` record.

Live 90 s check: `clockA` 2503 -> 5569 (ms), position steady at the live spawn
(143, -34), 45/50 B alternating exactly as the native does, no `0x15`.

The one field still carried rather than derived is the trailing float `v`
(686.5 -> 803.5 over the first seconds, then flat). It is not a clock and not
the position; `kDefaultV` holds the measured steady value.

### 700 s with the fixes: the reap is still there, and it is not a stream we omit

With the 37-byte `0xA6` at 60.03/s (240/s clock) and the live `0x83/0x05`
(position + ms clock), a 700 s solo run measured:

    wire t=473s tx=68085 rx=46348 srv_ack=17834 clk=11166 fail=0
    wire t=474s tx=68429 rx=46658 srv_ack=17867 clk=11166 fail=0
    raknet: server silent for 14s — session is dead
    ...
    wire t=88s  tx=76076 rx=49206 srv_ack=18543 clk=11959 fail=0
    raknet: server silent for 18s — session is dead      (89 s after rejoining)

Two things this rules out:

* **Not our send cadence.** `tx` and `srv_ack` are both climbing in the last
  second (`+344` datagrams, `+33` acks) and `fail=0`.
* **Not a missing UI/physics stream.** `0x83/0x07` — the one stream we never
  send — is local GUI replication: late-session frames are literal
  `PlayerGui.ScreenGui.TextLabel` updates ("Regenerating Thieves Den...",
  "Backpack.Sword"), and one early frame carries a zstd blob naming the job
  (`game_13058_1818_9cf1c4b0-...-_default_23`). A headless client has no GUI
  state to report; the native sends it because it draws.

The last thing the server does is a burst of ~1 KB inbound `0xA1` character
state records, then it goes silent — no `0x15`, so it is not a protocol error
reply. The second death came only **89 s** after a fresh join with a freshly
fetched join script and ticket, against 474 s on the first session: the life
span shrinks with session age rather than with anything we send. That is the
signature the issue reports describe (a per-session token that ages out and an
integrity reap that does not notify), and it is not reachable from the streams
we can build without the token generator itself.

### A 0x83 frame is a container, and its records are the whole client plane

One frame carries `0x06` (physics), `0x0a` (acks), `0x05` (position), `0x07`
(GUI) and `0x23` (clock) records back to back, which is why per-id censuses
undercount: the native's 3.8/s of `0x06` is mostly *inside* frames counted as
`0x07` or `0x05` by their first byte.

## 2026-11-07 — 0x83/0a was an order of magnitude over the native

Measured with `RBX_DUMP_TXAPP` (55 s, place 1818, `kingdudely_alt`): 4,719
outbound app messages, of which 1,488 are 0x83 frames that *begin* with a 0x0a
record and carry ~11,931 items in total — **~238 items/s**. The cause is
visible in the sender: every 0x23 clock reply grafted up to eight 0x0a items
onto itself, and we were answering ~30 server ticks/s. The native's measured
rate over 700 s is **22.6/s**.

That is the largest upstream difference left, and an over-rate stream is one of
the few things a server can shed a session for without answering with a 0x15 —
which is exactly the shape of our reap (silence, no error).

The item now has its own schedule: one record per 44 ms (22.7/s) on the
reliable-ordered stream, cycling the local character's replicated parts, and the
clock frame carries only 0x23. `RBX_NO_0A=1` restores the old silent behaviour
for A/B runs.

Note on the tick rate: silencing 0x0a does **not** slow the server's clock
ticks — 5,762 0x83/23 messages in the first ~3 minutes (~32/s) with the stream
off. So our reply rate follows the server, it is not a feedback loop caused by
the 0x0a flood.

### 0x0a is load-bearing, and what it has to name

Three 700 s-class soaks on place 1818, same build apart from this stream:

    variant                                   last healthy tick
    no 0x0a at all (RBX_NO_0A=1)              < 180 s
    22.7/s cycling the character's parts       ~300 s
    238/s, eight items on every clock reply   ~474 s

That looked like a dose-response, but a fourth clean run with the
server-driven variant below died at ~200 s, so the ordering is not established:
these are single soaks and earlier sessions varied between 340 s and 605 s for
the same build. What *is* solid is the first row — with no 0x0a at all the
session dies well before 180 s, where every variant that sends it lives for
minutes. The flood was not the reap, and the acks are not optional.

The ack's own meaning settles what it should name — "I applied your update for
this instance up to <clock>" — so the item now carries the ref of the instance
whose server state was just applied. `LiveRuntime` queues those refs in
`takeAppliedRef()` (deduped, bounded), and the sender drains one per 44 ms,
falling back to the character's parts only when the queue is empty. With that,
a 45 s dump shows 1,016 items (**22.6/s**, the native's measured rate) over 93
distinct refs — the instances the RCC is actually updating, instead of a fixed
eight we chose.

Clean soak with that variant: healthy to t=120, silently reaped before t=240.
Worse than the flood (474 s) and worse than cycling the character's parts
(~300 s), on one run each — the refs the server updates are evidently not a
substitute for dense per-part coverage of the character the client owns. Next
experiment, in order: ack the assembly root alone at 22/s; then per-part rates
of 5, 10 and 20/s with n=3 each, since single soaks cannot rank 200/300/474 s
against the 340-605 s spread seen earlier.

## 2026-11-07 — the reap is entity-level: transport stays up

The last datagram the server sends before we declare the session dead is an
**ACK**, not data:

    d0 00 01 01 8b 4c 00 13 28 00 13 28 00     (13 B, ACK shape)

So the RCC is still reading and acknowledging everything we send — it just
stops sending replication. That is not a transport drop, not a decrypt failure
(there are none in the log), and not a 0x15: it is the server dropping *our
entity* while the connection stays open. It fits the ack0a dose-response from
the previous entry (no acks -> entity gone in under three minutes).

Immediately before the silence the server dumps a burst of large `0xA1` state
frames, then nothing — i.e. it finishes the state it has and never speaks to us
again.

Two corrections from this round:

* `local_player_ref()` always returned 0: it trusted `Players.LocalPlayer`,
  which is a client-side property nothing sets in a headless client. It now
  falls back to the Player whose `UserId` is ours.
* `RBX_A1_OWN=1` reproduced `0x15 000104` (receive error) at ~45 live frames in
  three consecutive runs; the plain build was clean in the same window. The
  probe stays off by default; whatever ref A1 needs, it is not the one we were
  passing.

Default `0x83/0a` is the character's own parts at 22.7/s (`RBX_0A_SERVER=1`
switches to the server-record ref).

## 2026-11-07 — IDA: what the 0xA1 sender actually writes

`[DFLog::ISRIncomingOutgoingAttributes] InstanceStateSender
writeInstanceStatePacketHeader` (string 0x473f80 → `sub_55F7CA3`, caller
`sub_55FD3D6` per-object path `sub_55FDD3E`) writes:

    <lod-delta field>            sub_55FDDEF, value = lod + lod/2
    0xA1                         the app id, written after that field
    u32  ts                      the timestamp argument
    u32  0
    u32  <extra>                 only when flags & 0x40000000

So the two u32s after the A1 id are `ts` and a **zero**, with a third u32 gated
on a flag the sender reads from the replica (`vtable+64`). Our captured body has
`01 00 00 00` in the second slot, which is therefore either the object count
written after the header or a capture whose flag was set — it is not a constant
we can keep reproducing by rote.

The packet then serialises the object queue (`(ptr,float ts)` pairs, LOD from
`(count)` via `_BitScanReverse64`) through `InstanceStateSerializationManager`,
which is where the per-object ref comes from. That is the piece that decides
which instance an outbound A1 names; the probe results (Player / character /
root part all rejected with 0x15 0104) say it is not simply "any live ref".

## 2026-11-07 — 0x83/0x06 decoded and sent: the stream we never had

`tools/tx_census.py` on the r700d same-job round names the largest remaining
structural gap. Native game epoch (813 s) against ours (195 s), per second:

| kind | native /s | ours /s | note |
|------|----------:|--------:|------|
| 0xA6 input        | 59.08 | 31.61 | fixed after this capture (now 59.6) |
| 0x83/0x0A acks    | 21.45 | 21.90 | rate matches, **shape did not** |
| 0x83/0x23 clock   | 14.81 |  0.00 | see overshoot below |
| 0x83/0x06         |  3.80 |  0.00 | **never sent** |
| 0x83/0x05 pair    |  2.73 |  1.45 | |
| 0x83/0x0E         |  0.74 |  0.00 | never sent |
| 0x83/0x1C         |  0.71 |  0.00 | never sent |
| 0x83/0x07         |  0.25 |  0.00 | gated opener |
| 0x00              |  0.60 |  0.00 | never sent |
| 0xA1              |  0.31 |  0.00 | gated (ref semantics) |

The 0x0A note matters: our frames were **111 B each** (eight records per frame)
against the native's 16–35 B (one or two), i.e. ~175 instance-acks/s against
~32/s. Independent of the reap, that is 5x the upstream the native produces, so
"one record per 44 ms" is the right shape and the 111 B frames in r700d predate
that change.

### What 0x06 actually is

All 3,091 records in the capture share one 49-byte shape, and parsing it with
the `parseItem05` layout (type 0x06 takes the same reader) pins the fields:

```
83 06 01 | <u64 t> | <u32 t & 0xffffff>
         | <u32 t0> <u32 t1> <u32 t2> | <u32 n> <u32 m> <i32 lag>
         | <u32 0> | <u32 e> | <u8 tail> | 00
```

Regressing each field against datagram send time settles the epochs:

* `t` advances at **1000.07 units/s** and runs ~5,754 s ahead of the session
  clock, i.e. it is the client **process** clock in ms, not time since join.
* `t0/t1/t2` advance at **1000.01/s** in the session epoch, and `t2` is
  byte-identical to the clock of an adjacent 0x05 record **in the same frame**.
  The triple is therefore the (sample, receive, apply) times for the physics
  state 0x05 reports, and it links the two records.
* `lag` is that clock negated and sign-extended through 24 bits.
* `n`, `m` stay in 0..0x10; `d` is 0 in all 3,091; `e` is only ever 0 or
  `0xffffffff`. `n`/`m` are the two fields whose meaning is not recovered, so
  the builder takes them as arguments and the client passes 0 rather than
  inventing a count it does not keep.

`ack83::ack06()` writes that layout and `raknet_protocol_test` asserts it
reproduces a captured record byte-for-byte from its fields.

### Measured after wiring it at the native cadence

`RBX_RAKNET_TXDUMP` + `tx_census.py`, 95 s, place 1818:

```
a6      59.57/s  p50 16ms   15,18,21,34,37      native 59.08/s
83.23   26.70/s  p50 28ms   7                   native 14.81/s   <- 1.8x
83.0a   22.56/s  p50 44ms   14                  native 21.45/s   16-35B
83.05    5.63/s  p50 92ms   45,50               native  2.73/s   <- 2.1x
83.06    3.67/s  p50 268ms  49                  native  3.80/s   49B
```

Two 420 s soaks, same account and place, control then `RBX_06=1`: **both ran
the full window with no reap, no `0x15`, no rejoin.** So 0x06 is safe to send
and is now on by default (`RBX_NO_06=1` disables), but this pair does not show
it *fixing* the reap either — the control did not reap in that window, and a
340–605 s spread cannot be ranked by one run per arm.

### The next two numbers to move

1. **0x83/0x23 at 26.7/s against 14.8/s.** We answer every inbound clock tick
   plus a 1 s floor; the native answers about two of every three, with a p90 gap
   of 132 ms. Over-rate upstream is the one thing a server can shed a session
   for, so this is the highest-value rate fix left.
2. **0x83/0x05 at 5.63/s against 2.73/s** — the same ~2x overshoot on the frame
   that keeps the 300 s liveness timer fed.

Both are cadence, not grammar: the frames themselves are byte-correct.

### The 0x05 overshoot was a duplicated send, not a cadence

After the 0x06 work the census still showed `0x83/0x05` at 4.70/s (45 B) and 0.93/s
(50 B) against the native's 2.23 and 0.50. Two runs with the gate at 500 ms and
900 ms fitted `rate ≈ 2.81/s + 945/interval_ms`, i.e. a constant ~2.8/s source that
had nothing to do with the gate, and `RBX_NO_ACK83=1` still produced 158 45 B
frames in 58 s (2.7/s) — so the block in `raknet_main` was not the only sender.

It is `LiveRuntime`'s physics stream: every 0.7 s it built one 45-byte frame and
sent **the same buffer twice**, once unreliable and once reliable.
`2 × (1/0.7) = 2.86/s`, which is the fitted constant. The native never does
this — its 45 B form is unreliable at 2.23/s and the reliable copy is the
50-byte form at 0.50/s.

Dropping the second send, with the gate left at 900 ms:

| | before | after | native |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x05 45B | 4.70/s | **2.98/s** (2.98 total) | 2.23/s |
| 0x05 50B | 0.93/s | 0.52/s | 0.50/s |

### 0x83/0x23 is still over, and the ratio is the honest measure

Our clock replies track inbound ticks, so the absolute rate depends on the job:
33.85/s in this window against the native's 14.81/s. Absolute rates across
different jobs are not comparable; the job-independent measure is the ratio to
the per-instance acks, which the same activity drives:

* native `23 : 0a` = 14.81 : 21.45 = **0.69**
* ours   `23 : 0a` = 33.85 : 22.53 = **1.50**

So we answer roughly twice as many ticks per ack as the native. We reply to
every inbound tick plus a 1 s floor; the native answers about two in three.
Which ticks it selects is not recovered, so a 2/3 duty cycle would be a rate
match with an invented rule behind it — the next step is a same-job round
(`tools/parity_round.sh`) so both sides see the same tick stream and the
selection can be read off instead of guessed.

### On 304 (answered from public sources, not measured here)

`304` is `AndroidAnticheatKick` and `318` is the Android Remote Attestation
failure; they are different verdicts. Roblox's own help page describes
attestation as hardware-backed and per-game, and Sober's tracker shows 318 only
on games that opt into ARA (RIVALS 17625359962), while Sober passes ordinary
games that a modified Android runtime would otherwise trip. So 304 is not an
attestation wall, and an Android runtime on Linux can pass it. Our client has
one measured 304, at session age 666.788 s, with no reproduction since; that is
still an unexplained single observation and the parity work above is what would
close it, not an attestation bypass.

## 2026-11-07 — the join identity: UA decides the platform, and it must agree with the 0x8a body

`gamejoin` mints `SessionId.PlatformTypeId` / `DeviceType` from the request's
`User-Agent` alone, so the header is not cosmetic: it decides which platform the
session is issued for, and the RakNet edge only serves sessions for platforms it
recognises.

A/B on place 1818, same account, same build, `RBX_JOIN_UA` the only difference:

| `RBX_JOIN_UA` | `PlatformTypeId` | `DeviceType` | outcome |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| `Roblox/WinInet` (was the default) | 5 | 1 (desktop) | joins, 2,853 instances |
| `Roblox/Android/2.743.1136` (now the default) | 30 | 3 (phone) | joins, 2,647 instances |
| `Roblox/2.739.691` | 8 | 1 | **no `OpenConnectionReply2` ever**, Req2 retries until timeout |
| a browser UA (`Mozilla/5.0 … Chrome/…`) | — | — | `join failed: status=12 "Unable to join Game 211"` |

Two consequences:

* **"It just doesn't connect" is reproducible without any protocol bug.** Any
  `User-Agent` that mints a platform the RakNet edge will not serve (8 above)
  dies at Req2 with no Reply2 — indistinguishable, from the client side, from a
  firewall. The UA is now one function (`rbx::userAgent()`) used by the join
  request, player hydration and the executor's `http_request`, so a script
  cannot make one process present two platforms.
* **The web client cannot be copied instead of the APK.** Weblox's finding
  (a browser UA needs `gamejoin` rewritten to `Roblox/WinInet` or it gets
  status 12) reproduces here directly: the browser arm never received a session
  at all. There is no "adopt the web platform and dodge the Android verdict"
  path through this endpoint.

### What the old default was, and why it was wrong

The client asked as `Roblox/WinInet` (platform 5, desktop) while the 0x8a join
body declares `"Android"` — a session that is desktop to the HTTP layer and
Android to the game server. No native client presents that pair: the Windows
client sends a Windows body, the Android client an Android one. The default now
matches the body, and `RBX_JOIN_UA` remains for A/B runs.

Whether this pair was the silent per-entity reap (previous sections) is being
measured in one shared job: two of our clients, one per identity, same job id,
700 s, first reap time compared. Result in the next entry.

## 2026-11-07 — 304 is deterministic at ~660 s, and the native survives it in the same job

The one-off 304 is not a one-off. Two of our clients joined one job
(`5124f1a2-…`), one with `RBX_JOIN_UA=Roblox/WinInet` (platform 5) and one with
the Android identity (platform 30), different accounts:

    control  0x15 disconnect (code 304 / 0x0130) at session age 660.038s
    android  0x15 disconnect (code 304 / 0x0130) at session age 660.030s
    payload  1500000130  (the 5-byte form; no preceding request)

Different account, different platform, same verdict at the same age to within
8 ms — so it is neither the UA nor the account. It is not a job-wide event
either: the native client, in a job our client was pinned to
(`9ede9669-…`, native `cookie.txt` + ours `king.txt`), kept a single epoch for
its whole 756 s window while ours took `0x15 304` at **661.295 s** in the same
server. Native captures on disk are all single-epoch 741-813 s, i.e. it is
never kicked in this window.

### What arrives before it

The last 40 inbound messages before the kick are 35 `0xA1` state records and a
run of `0x83/0x23` ticks, then the `0x15`. There is no challenge, no prompt and
no error frame to answer — the RCC decides on state it already has, ~11 minutes
in.

### What is still missing from our client at minute 10

Same-job window 540-660 s, native TX (ours was not dump-captured in this round):

| id | native /120 s | ours |
|---|---:|---|
| `0xA6` input | 7,192 | sends (59/s) |
| `0x83/0x0A` acks | 2,795 | sends |
| `0x83/0x23` clock | 1,624 | sends (~2x rate) |
| `0x83/0x06` | 456 | sends (since 2026-11-07) |
| `0x83/0x05` | 312 | sends |
| `0x83/0x1C` | 110 | **never** (GUI batch container) |
| `0x83/0x07` | 77 | **never** (local GUI replication) |
| `0x00` | 72 | **never** (transport-internal) |
| `0xA1` | 39 | **never** (InstanceStateSender) |
| `0x03` | 24 | never |
| `0x83/0x0E` | 6 | never (ack batch container) |

### The boundary

`304` is `DisconnectAndroidAnticheatKick`: the verdict, not a protocol error. A
client that produces the native's *entire* stream set (including the GUI state
reports and the instance-state sender) is legitimate parity work, and the
missing rows above are real gaps to close. Making a modified client pass an
integrity check by reproducing or forging the client's attestation token
(`SetTokenGenAlgo` / `SetTokenPepper` / `tokenGenerated` are all in
`libroblox.so`) is a different thing: that is defeating detection rather than
matching a protocol, so it is not implemented here.

What the client does in the meantime, measured in the same run: the 304 is
honoured and the session is rejoined twice with a fresh join script, and the
second session ran the rest of the window normally (`tx_fail=0`, clean census).
That is the correct behaviour for a session the server ends; it is not a crash.

## 2026-11-08 — 304 is not an Android verdict (the desktop/web identity gets it too)

The open question after the identity fix was whether the kick is specific to the
Android platform, and therefore whether the *web* client's identity — the one
Weblox uses — is less restricted. Measured, both halves:

**What Weblox actually presents.** `iwa/auth.js` rewrites `User-Agent` to
`Roblox/WinInet` for `gamejoin` only, through the controlled frame's request
interceptor (a browser UA gets `status 12, "Unable to join Game 211"`, so the
rewrite is what makes a join exist at all). `engine/WasmPlayer.js` then calls
`set_pretend_platform(navigator.userAgent.includes("Android") ? "mobile" :
"desktop")`, i.e. desktop here. Its transport is not WebTransport: `tools/patch.py`
splices the engine's sockets onto Direct Sockets and `iwa/ring.js`/`rupp.js`
carry RakNet plus the TLV1/TLV2 stamping, so the RCC sees a genuine RakNet
client with a **Windows** session. The module also carries the same UA table
(`Roblox/WinInet`, `Roblox/XboxOne`, `Roblox/Android`, `Roblox/WinPCGDK`) and
`SetTokenGenAlgo`, so nothing about the web build skips the token path.

**The A/B in our client.** Same account, place 1818, `--headless`:

    RBX_JOIN_UA=Roblox/WinInet RBX_JOIN_PLATFORM=Windows

    join: session platform PlatformTypeId=5 DeviceType=1
    join: 0x8a platform=Windows ua=Roblox/WinInet
    0x9b challenge answered, 35 player-joined records, replication flowing
    raknet: 0x15 disconnect (code 304 / 0x0130) at session age 660.002s

660 s, to the millisecond, on the desktop identity: the same instant as the
Android runs. So 304 is not platform-gated, and copying Weblox's identity does
not avoid it — the verdict is about what the client does during the session, not
about which platform it claims to be.

**Identity consistency, permanently.** The 0x8a body's platform used to be the
literal `"Android"` while the UA could be anything, so a desktop-minted session
declared Android to the RCC — an identity no real client presents. The body now
takes its platform from `rbx::joinPlatform()`, which derives it from the same UA
(`WinInet`/`WinPCGDK` -> Windows, `Android` -> Android), with `RBX_JOIN_PLATFORM`
for A/B runs. Default output is unchanged (Android), which is why the captured
0x8a golden vector still matches byte for byte.

## 2026-11-08 — The 0x23 clock is a 240 Hz tick, and we were running it 16.7x fast

Censusing the native and this client over the same 415 s of one session
(`run/parity/r700d`, both connections in the same job) gives the send gap that
matters for parity, in messages/s:

    id        native   ours          id        native   ours
    a6         58.4     38.8          83.0e     1.07    0
    83.0a      20.7      8.7          83.1c     0.79    0
    83.23      14.0      0.0*         00        0.60    0.13
    83.06       3.8      0.0*         a1        0.29    0.0*
    83.05       2.7      1.8          83.07     0.39    0

(*) present in that capture only once, at join, or not at all. Total TX rate:
**102.9/s native vs 49.9/s ours**.

`0x83/23` is the one stream that is a pure clock, so it is the first thing that
had to be exactly right. Two measurements on the 813 s native capture:

* 11445 of the 12044 `0x83/23` frames are the 7-byte shape
  `83 23 <u32 tick> 00`; the tick rises **304 -> 193804 over 813 s = 239.1/s**,
  i.e. a 240 Hz clock, not milliseconds.
* the same value appears in the `0xA1` packet header (`a1 <tick> ...`) and in
  the `23` item of the `0x83/04` ready frame, whose captured bytes are
  `83 04 d6 09 00 00 | 23 62 00 00 00 | 00` — 2518 **ms** and tick **98**, not
  one number written twice.

Both of our builders wrote the millisecond clock into the tick slot, and the
app-layer sender wrote `ms * 4` = 4000 ticks/s, i.e. a client clock running
16.7x faster than the real one's and one that drifts against the RCC's own time
by ~10 minutes an hour. `batch::net_tick_240()` is now the single tick domain
(derived from `net_clock_ms()` so it shares the origin and the world-live
rebase), and `ready_batch`/`ready_with_transform` take the tick separately from
the millisecond clock.

**Cadence, measured rather than assumed.** The native emits this frame at
**14.8/s** for the whole session. Answering every server clock record ran us at
**34.0/s** (the server sends clock records faster than the native answers), and
the first cut of a free-running 16-tick gate ran at 40/s because `pump()` is not
called once per frame and the gate was a plain static shared by several threads.
One frame per 67 ms on the app layer, which owns the stream, measures
**12.7/s at 240.0 ticks/s with zero duplicate ticks** over 95 s — the tick slope
now matches the native's 239.1 and the cadence is within 15%.

**The web client cannot be an app-level oracle.** With Weblox's IWA live in a
game (place 13569002876, 55 fps, 58 ms ping, 18.5k datagrams sent), the
`Rupp`/ring seam exposes every outgoing datagram, but the payload after the
31-byte TLV block is sealed: the byte after the TLV block is uniform over
0x00..0xff across 1050 datagrams and no `0x84..0x8f` datagram id appears at any
offset. So it confirms the desktop identity joins and stays connected, and it
gives a datagram-level rate, but it yields no application ids. Parity work stays
on native captures and IDA.

## 2026-11-08 — What the web client actually presents, read out of its live engine

The question was whether this client should stop claiming Android and copy the
web client instead. Measured on the IWA that is signed in and in-game on this
machine (`chrome://iwa-dev`, CDP on 9223), against the same question in the
native APK:

* **The web engine carries the same UA table as the APK** — read out of linear
  memory at `0x4664xx`: `Roblox/WinInet`, `Roblox/XboxOne ROBLOX Xbox App`,
  `Roblox/Android`, `Roblox/WinPCGDK ROBLOX PCGDK App 1.0.0`. It is not a
  browser identity with a RakNet client bolted on.
* **The live UA string it built is `Roblox/WinInet`** (a runtime copy at
  `0x2004d27`, next to the session constants), and `WasmPlayer.js` calls
  `set_pretend_platform("desktop")` on any non-Android UA. So the web session is
  platform 5 / device 1 — exactly the pair this client can already produce with
  `RBX_JOIN_UA=Roblox/WinInet RBX_JOIN_PLATFORM=Windows`.
* **Its build identity cannot be copied.** A scan of the whole 2 GiB heap for
  the join constant shape `<40 hex>!<32 hex>.` returns nothing, and no
  `0xC001CAFE` terminator anywhere: the web build does not use the APK's 0x8a
  body at all. Presenting its UA while sending the APK's constants is the mixed
  identity that was already fixed once (0x8a platform is derived from the UA for
  this reason), so the Android identity stays the default.

**And the identity is not what the verdict is about.** The same desktop pair was
already A/B'd (`RBX_JOIN_UA=Roblox/WinInet RBX_JOIN_PLATFORM=Windows`, place
1818): it joins, answers 0x9b, replicates, and still takes `0x15 304` at
**660.002 s**. The real web client, meanwhile, sat in a place for roughly an
hour and was removed only by the **20-minute idle rule** (`Error Code: 278`,
screenshot on disk); it is not idle-kicked at 11 minutes and it is never given
304. A genuine client survives; this one does not. The remaining difference is
in what we send, not in what we claim to be.

## 2026-11-08 — One client put two 0x83/05 shapes on the wire

Censusing the live TX of a 700 s run showed *two* 45-byte 0x83/05 streams from
one client, with different layouts:

    ack83::ack05 (app layer)   83 05 16 | a<u24> 00 00000000 00 | a 00 00000000 00 | x z 0 | 0 | fl | b 00 | v | 00
    physics_batch_clocked     83 05 16 | a<u32> 00000000 b<u32> | x y z | 0 | flags | 01 | c | f | 00

Same message id, same length, different meaning: in the first the third float is
a constant 0 and the pair is the *horizontal* position, in the second the middle
float is the vertical. Re-deriving the frame from the 811 s native capture settles
which is right — the reference's first frame is

    83 05 16 1b160000 00000000 1b160000 00000000 | 1b91fa41 349f6a41 00000000
    | 00000000 | 01 | 1c160000 | 80a12b44 | 00

i.e. x=31.3209 y=14.6639 z=0 at **offset 19**, `a == b` climbing ~997/s (one
millisecond clock sampled twice), `c` within +-7 ms of it, two flag fields (0 or
0xffffffff, and 0 or 1) that are uncorrelated, and a trailing float in 618..878
across ten captures that is never 0 and whose meaning is still unresolved.

Fixes: `physics_batch_clocked` now writes the reference order (and `unit_batch`
pins that exact frame, plus decodes x/y/z back out of offsets 19/23/27 so a
reorder cannot pass by matching only the length); the duplicate upload inside
`LiveRuntime` is gone, so the app layer's `ack83::ack05` is the single sender
(it is the one that also emits the 50-byte reliable copy with a 0x23 record);
and the dead `physics_chain` builder, which shared the wrong layout, is deleted.

Also repaired: `unit_exec` and `unit_engine` no longer link. `exec.cpp`'s
`http_request` asks with `rbx::userAgent()` (rbx_join) and the two test targets
only linked rbx_solve, so the tree failed to build at HEAD with
`undefined reference to rbx::userAgent()`. They link rbx_join now. 22/22 ctest
pass.

**One new verdict, seen once:** a run took `0x15 268`
(`DisconnectOnRemoteSysStats`) at session age 4.337 s while a `-j$(nproc)` build
was saturating the machine. A 120 s control run on the same build joined and
stayed connected, so it is not a join-path regression; the name points at
system-stats reporting, and contending every core during a session is the
obvious suspect. It needs a deliberate reproduction (idle machine vs. loaded)
before anything is concluded from it.

## 2026-11-08 — the whole 0xA1 gap is one value tag, and the old pointer to it was wrong

Engine-parity sweep on this build (place 1818): **every executed LocalScript
succeeds.** `ClassicLoadingScreen` (6301 B) runs clean headless; the failures
recorded in older logs (`Font.new`, `ReplicatedFirst.RemoveDefaultLoadingScreen`,
`PlayerGui.ChildAdded`) are all closed. Blox Fruits (2753915549) joins, applies
~62k instances, and its 6517 B `Boot` chunk runs clean too. So the Luau API
surface is not what stops real places — nothing in these runs failed on a missing
global, service, or event.

What *is* missing is property application. On 1818, 25.7% of 0xA1 records fail
(14,595 of 56,650 attempted member applications), and the census attributes
**100% of those failures to one value tag**:

    a1why="tag 57 (Part.ReplicationPV) ...":9598
          "tag 57 (MeshPart.ReplicationPV) ...":4997   -> 14,595 = a1fail

A failed value read abandons the *whole record* (`parseAppStateBody` returns
false), so every member after the offending one in that record is lost too. The
code refuses tag 57 on the stated grounds that it is client->server-only and
"never sent server->client" — contradicted by every one of these sessions.

Two corrections to the record:

1. The shape note pointed at `sub_3615946`. IDA resolves that address inside
   `sub_3614F2C`, which is the **AdService rewarded-video handler** — unrelated to
   replication. That note has been wrong since it was written, which is why tag
   57 never got solved: the lead led nowhere.
2. `ReplicationPV` lengths are not constant. Sample leftovers after the value
   (tag 57 as the last selected member, so leftover == value length) measured
   14 B; with `RotVelocity` (tag 22, 12 B) after it, the same member measured
   10 B. So it is a kind/flag-prefixed struct, not a fixed-width value. The
   411-byte leftover case shows some of these records also carry a trailing
   section this grammar does not model at all.

Tooling fix, because every offline grammar tool was lying about which member it
was looking at: `RBX_DUMP_SCHEMA` now writes the per-class **slot table** from
the 0x97 tail (`  s <slot> <global member id>`), and `tools/a1_tail_probe.cpp`
parses it and rebuilds `member_by_gid`, so an offline probe resolves a mask slot
through the writer's list exactly like `parseAppStateBody` does. Before this, the
probe fell back to direct member indexing and reported *Humanoid.TargetPoint
(tag 22) truncated* for bodies that production decodes fine — a phantom gap that
looked like a Vector3 width bug. With slots, the same 4,023-record capture shows
3 failures, all genuine tag 57.

Slots round-trip exactly: 6,401 ids across 638 classes, matching the live tail's
`ids summing to 6,401 of 10,328 members`.

Still open: the tag-57 struct itself (kind byte + variable body), and the
trailing section behind the 411-byte record.

## 2026-11-09 — no captured 0x90 body is needed (the "missing template" blocker)

The 0x90 join body used to require `run/raknet90_template.bin`, a capture of a
signed-in session. That is what made a fresh clone unable to join, and it leaked
another account's material. Measured, it was never necessary.

Payload shape: `[9-byte header][flag list][LEB(JSON)][JSON][24-byte tail]`.
Only the JSON carries session identity.

- Header `01 24 00 00 00 03 0e <u16 flag count>`: build constants (36, 14).
- Flag list: LEB-length-prefixed names, e.g. `UserPlayerScriptsSAuthDirectAPIs2`.
- Tail: 24 bytes, and `tail[2]` feeds `key8a()` — build constants, not session.

The tail and header are therefore *build* constants and now live in
`rbxmsg::default90Template()`; the document is filled by `build90()` from the
join script (UserId / UserName / AccountAge / RandomSeed1 / APIsecurityToken /
ticket) plus the hydration payload (AgeBracket / Gender / Os / Platform /
IsOriginalUser / OriginalAccountCreationTimestampMs). Derived fields that used to
silently keep the *capture's* values are now built: `CountryRegionCode` from the
join script's `CountryCode`, `LocaleId`/`LocaleIdInternal` from `RobloxLocale`,
`CharacterAppearance` from place id + user id, and `ClientJoinData` from
`LaunchData`/`ReferredByPlayerId`.

**The flag list is per-account, not per-build.** A second native capture (a
different account, older build) carried **44** flags of which 6 names do not
appear in this build's 48, and 1 appears only there. The names are A/B cohort
labels (`UserPlayerScriptsImprovementA/B/C/D`, `UserAbilitiesUserInterfaceC…H`,
`CCLIntegrationA…H`), so the native computes them from the account's experiment
assignment. It is capability reporting: the RCC accepts either set, which is why
the generated 48-name list joins fine. Exact per-account reproduction of this
list is the one remaining unknown in the 0x90 body; it does not gate a join.

Live proof, with `run/raknet90_template.bin` and `run/raknet8a_template.bin`
moved away (log: `no 0x90 template … using the built-in build-constant body`):

| place | 0x90 body | 0x9B | 0x97 | world |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1818 | 10,883 B (json 9,367) | solved in 8 ms | 54,254 B | 2,593 instances, ChatGGPT in the player list |
| 2753915549 (Blox Fruits) | 11,002 B (json 9,486) | solved | 36,334 B | 2,041 instances, 60 s clean |

Both ran the full window with no `0x15` disconnect. 2753915549 is also the
`key8a()` regression case (place id ≥ 2^31 formats as signed), so the
community-reported `0x15 / 0x0106` (`DisconnectSendPacketError`) on large place
ids is covered by the same runs.

Regression coverage lives at the top of `client/tests/join_msg_test.cpp` and runs
on a clean tree (the capture sections still skip): header, 48-flag walk, exact
byte accounting, 53-key document, and every derived identity field.

## 2026-11-09 — tag 57 no longer costs a record (0xA1)

`BasePart.ReplicationPV` (tag 57) is `RobloxSecurity`, `Hidden` and
`NotScriptable`: no game script can read it, and its value is per-part physics
state that a client which does not simulate cannot use. But refusing it used to
throw away the **whole record**, including every member decoded before it —
13,599 of 63,675 member applications in a 55 s run, 100% of A1 failures.

The 0xA1 record carries its own body length, so the value does not need a shape
to be skipped exactly:

- **tag 57 is the record's last selected member** — the body length says where
  it ends, so the remainder is consumed and the record closes exactly. In a 45 s
  capture that is 948 of 1,067 tag-57 records.
- **members follow tag 57** — their offsets are unknowable without its width, so
  the members decoded *before* it are kept and the record is marked partial,
  the same contract the reader already used for an unknown mask slot.

Measured on place 1818 (traffic differs per session, so the denominators are
given; `a1part` is a *subset* of the applied records — a partial record is still
an applied one):

| | records | applied | a1fail | applied % |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| before | 69,570 | 41,973 | 26,071 | 60.3% |
| after | 54,610 | 53,026 (10,213 partial) | **0** | 97.1% |

The rest are records skipped for an unusable ref (`!r.id` / null sentinel),
which the native ignores as well. The world still builds (3,184 instances) and
the loading-screen script still runs 1/0/0.

A width sweep over 1,067 tag-57 records (`tools/a1_57_width.cpp`, which requires
the rest of the record to close exactly) gives no single width: 22, 19, 14 and 6
all occur, and `last`-position extents range 24–66 B. So it is a flag/kind
prefixed variable struct, and the earlier `sub_3615946` pointer (which IDA
resolves inside the AdService handler, i.e. unrelated) is not the reader. The
value itself remains undecoded, which is observable to nobody; the *record* is
no longer lost, which was observable as missing parts.

### Multi-place join sweep, capture-free build (2026-11-09)

Same account, no `run/raknet*_template.bin` present, 35 s windows:

| place | 0x9B | world | scripts | a1fail |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1818 Crossroads | solved | 3,184 instances | 1 / 0 | 0 |
| 2753915549 Blox Fruits | solved | 2,041 | 1 / 0 | 0 |
| 920587237 Adopt Me | solved | 1,759 | 1 / 0 | 0 |
| 142823291 Murder Mystery 2 | solved | 27,744 | 6 / 0 | 0 |
| 189707 Natural Disaster Survival | solved | 9,516 | 0 / 0 | 0 |

Adopt Me is the one that previously "never delivered 0x97 within 35 s, so that
world never applied" — the join-path gap is gone with the generated body. Every
place reaches a world, and none has an A1 record failure.

## 2026-11-09 — the binary in ~/rr is 2.743, and what the 0xA1 extra mask bit is

### Which binary the notes were written against

`~/rr/libroblox.so` is `2.743.0.1136` (md5 `30ecaff9…`), and IDA's database has
the same md5 — the database and the file agree. The *notes* in this file that
name `2.739.0.691` were written against the older `libroblox-2.739.0.691.so`
that still sits next to it, so a symbol or address quoted from those notes is
one build behind. Two things move with the version and both are now on 2.743:

* the join body's `ClientRobloxVersion` is `2.743.1136` (the JSON form drops
  the zero minor component: 2.739's full string was `2.739.0.691` and the
  capture said `2.739.691`), and
* the join UA must agree with it, because the join service mints
  `PlatformTypeId`/`DeviceType` from the UA alone and the 0x8a body says
  `Android`. A live join now logs
  `join: 0x8a platform=Android ua=Roblox/Android/2.743.1136`.

### Found on a live 2.743 stream: mask bit `index == named` is the attribute flag

Over 2,000 dumped frames (25,517 records, place 1818, `--place-id 1818
--seconds 360`), 3,874 records (15%) leave bytes after their selected members.
Every one of them has exactly one mask bit set at `index == <named slots>`,
never higher and never more than one:

| class | named slots | bit set |
|---|---|---|
| Part | 56 | 56 |
| MeshPart | 68 | 68 |
| Humanoid | 48 | 48 |
| Model | 10 | 10 |
| Tool | 15 | 15 |
| Animation | 4 | 4 |
| Folder | 6 | 6 |
| Lighting | 23 | 23 |

So the flag is "this record carries an attribute section", not a member: the
body is the member values in ascending order followed by the section. The
section starts `00 <type> <u32 name length> <name>` and the value follows the
name; strings carry a 1-byte length (`00 0c 05 "Batch"`, `... "State" 04
"None"`, `... "MouseCursor" 21 rbxasset://textures/GunCursor.png` where 0x21 is
33 = the string's length). `tools/a1_attr_probe.py` walks dumped tails with
that shape and scores exact closure, which is how the value widths get fitted;
`tools/a1_tail_probe` (`A1_TAIL_DIR=<dir>`) and `tools/a1_frame_trace.py` are
the census and framing front ends.

At the time of that run the member prefix of those records *was* applied and
the record counted `a1part`, but the attribute section was not modelled.  That
work was completed on 2026-11-15; see the final `0xA1 attribute replication and
null-ref boundaries` section for the current layout and measurements.

### Two smaller parser fixes from the same run

* A mask with a **zero-length body** is a record the RCC selected members for
  but sends no values for (`AnimationClipProvider.RobloxLocked`, same
  RobloxSecurity family as `BasePart.ReplicationPV`). It closed as a failure
  and cost the record; it is now closed as a no-op (`a1fail` went to 0 on a
  120 s slice).
* The record-boundary scan's first byte is the start of the record ref varint,
  not an enum — the old `{1,2,4}` test only accepted ids 1, 2 and 4, so any
  boundary that reached the scan was lost whenever the next record's id was
  something else. Desyncs are now rare (11 frames in ~5,500) rather than tied
  to the next record's id, and such a frame is still dropped whole and counted
  rather than half-applied.

### The boundary that was missing then: records with a null ref

About 8% of record boundaries (1,664 of 20,375 measured over the same 2,012
