frames) open a record whose `ref lo` is 0. Those records carry state with no
instance id, so the head is 4 bytes shorter (no u32 id follows the varint) and
a probe that always assumes the 5-byte ref reads the class four bytes late,
declares the boundary invalid, and falls back to the previous record's body
end — which is how a frame gets mis-locked and then loses its tail.

Accepting the null-ref head as another valid layout does fix those frames (9 of
the 11 dumped failures walk again), but it is net worse: because it is a
*shorter* head it also validates at boundaries that already hold a normal head
four bytes earlier, locking the walk early and desyncing 12 frames that
otherwise walked. Trying the normal head first everywhere and the null-ref head
only as a last resort measured 24 losses, and preferring it at `body_end + 4`
measured 25, against 12 for rejecting it outright:

| boundary probe | frames lost of 2,012 |
|---|---|
| 5-byte head only, first byte 0 rejected (current) | 12 |
| also accept null-ref head, preferred | 25 |
| also accept null-ref head, last resort | 24 |

That framing gap was not a missing constant: local header validity could not
tell the two alignments apart.  The fix now in the tree decodes the candidate's
body at the exact boundary (`parseAppStateBody` closes on exactly one of them)
and only then accepts the null layout; the earlier permissive variants below
are the measurements that ruled out accepting it everywhere.

`tools/a1_gap_stats.py` is the measurement front end for that work: it walks
the dumped frames, records the gap at every boundary, and correlates it with
the ending record's body length, class and top mask bit.

### Two disambiguators that measured worse, so they are not in the tree

The obvious next idea — require the candidate record's body to decode before
accepting a boundary — was implemented and measured, in three forms, on the
same 2,012 frames (the baseline loses 12):

| boundary rule | frames lost |
|---|---|
| 5-byte head only, first byte 0 rejected | 12 |
| accept both head layouts, body must decode (partial allowed) | 23 |
| accept both layouts, body must close strictly (no partial) | 1,056 |
| candidate order normal/null x +4/+0, strict then structural | 81 |

The reason is that 15% of real records are *partial* by design (their hash ends
in a member the slot list does not name, i.e. the attribute section), so strict
closure rejects correct boundaries, and permissive closure also accepts wrong
ones — a four-byte shift keeps the mask and body length plausible. Body content
only becomes a usable signal once the attribute section is modelled, so the
framing work is blocked on that; the probe stays at the measured best until
then.

## 2026-11-09 — 700 s same-job round on the current build: volume matches, the body does not

One round, place 1818, one job (`90d4661a…`), native under gdb and ours pinned to it
with `--job-id`, 700 s window (`tools/parity_round.sh`, `run/parity/r7e1`). This is
the first round after the 60 Hz input fix and the 0x06/0x0A work, and it moves the
comparison from "rates are wrong" to "one stream's payload is missing".

### Rates now

| stream | ours /s | native /s | verdict |
|--------|--------:|----------:|---------|
| 0xA6 input | 59.93 | 59.69 | matches |
| 0x83/0x06 | 3.70 | 3.82 | matches |
| 0x83/0x0A | 22.70 | 9.89 | frame count 2.3x, record count not comparable (framing) |
| 0x83/0x23 | 13.07 | 24.06 | under; ours are all 7 B, native's are 7–24 B |
| 0x83/0x05 | 1.64 | 3.09 | under by ~half |
| 0x83/0x0E | 0 | 1.42 | **never sent** |
| 0x83/0x07 | 0 | 0.94 | gated (canned opener, see below) |
| 0x00 | 0.19 | 0.60 | under |
| 0x83/0x1C | 0 | 0.36 | **never sent** |
| 0xA1 | 0 | 0.18 | **never sent** (gated on ref semantics) |

Frame counts for 0xA6 and 0x83 now agree to within a few percent, which is what the
earlier rounds were fighting. What does *not* agree is bytes: ours 2.9 kB/s upstream
against the native's 8.2 kB/s.

### The whole byte gap is 0xA6, and it is the character's own transforms

Native 0xA6 is 7,022 B/s over 59.69 frames/s, i.e. ~117 B per frame, and the frame
length histogram says why:

    141 B x 37,726      <- the steady frame, not 37 B
     37 B x 10,907      <- the short form we send
     34 B x 10, 15/28/31/63/89/115 x 1

The 141-byte frame keeps the header we already reproduce (`a6 04 01 00 10 <13-byte
route> <u32 step> <u32 step> 00 00`) and then carries back-to-back 19-byte records:

    02 7f | 5d 01 00 | 04 00 | 05 7d 44 00 c5 e0 43
    11    | 5d 01 00 | 04 00 | 05 7d 44 00 c5 e0 43

`5d 01 00` is the u24 ref `0x015d05`, the same instance the native's 0xA1 frames
name, and the trailing 7 bytes move with it. So the native reports its own
character's per-part transforms in the input stream at 60 Hz, and the client that
sends only the 37-byte frame is telling the server nothing about where it is. That
is the largest remaining upstream difference and it subsumes the 0xA1 gap: both
streams name the same ref.

This is *not* implemented, deliberately. The 7-byte tail is not yet decoded, and a
plausible-looking wrong guess here is worse than an absent stream: the earlier
probe that addressed 0xA1 with a Player/character/root-part ref was answered with
`0x15 01 04`, so the RCC validates whatever this is.

### 304, still exactly where it was

    raknet: 0x15 AndroidAnticheatKick (code 304 / 0x0130) at session age 660.003s

in this round, on this build, with A6 at the native rate and 0x83 at 78% of it. The
native client in the same job ran the full 820 s window on one connection. So the
verdict does not track input rate, message volume, account, job or endpoint — the
remaining measured difference is the payload above.

Two smaller differences are recorded because they are cheap to fix and currently
unexplained: we send the six pre-join clock pings (0x51–0x56) ~133 times each
*inside the game epoch* where the native sends 0–5, and we send no 0x00 frames at
0.6/s.

The run also ended in `rc=139` (SIGSEGV) after the 660 s kick: the client had begun
a rejoin when the harness's SIGINT arrived at 700 s. A crash on shutdown is its own
defect and is not yet diagnosed.

## 2026-11-09 — the 0x51-0x56 probes are answered under RakNet's reply id

The same-job round left six ids in our TX that the native never sends in the game
epoch (0x51-0x56, 134 of each over 700 s), so the obvious reading was that the
mirror should stop once the app layer is up. That was wrong, and the control run
says why:

| arm | what it does | outcome |
|-----|--------------|---------|
| mirror echo (old) | answer each probe with the probe's own id | live to 660 s, 134 of each id in TX |
| handshake-only | answer nothing once `app_started` | **server silent at 237 s**, rejoin |
| reply id (new) | 0x51-0x53 -> `0x00`, 0x54-0x56 -> `0x03` | live past 420 s, 0x00 0.80/s, 0x03 0.23/s |

So the answer is mandatory and it is not the probe's id. The native's own counts
fit the same conclusion: 0x51-0x53 zero times and 0x54-0x56 five times, against
488x 0x00 (0.60/s) and 159x 0x03 (0.20/s) over 815 s, while the RCC probes at
0.58/s. It was answering all along, under RakNet's connected-ping/pong ids; what
it never does is echo the probe id back.

The 420 s soak after the change shows no `0x15`, no silence, and no probe id in
TX. What it does not show is whether the 660 s verdict moved: 0x00 at 0.80/s is
slightly above the native's 0.60/s, and that is the next number to tune.

### The 660 s verdict does not depend on the probe handling

720 s soak on the build that answers 0x51-0x53 with `0x00` and 0x54-0x56 with
`0x03` (the native's ids), place 1818, same account:

    190279  app message 0x15 (5B) 1500000130      <- 0x0130 = 304
    ...     t=660s live: ... 304 arrived, stats printed, server silent, rejoin

So the probe ids were a real parity error (134 of each, ~800 messages the native
never sends) and fixing them did not move the verdict. Combined with the 700 s
round, the things 304 does *not* track are now: account, job, endpoint, platform
identity, input rate, total message volume, and the probe reply ids.

What is left is the one stream whose payload we still cannot produce: the
native's 141-byte 0xA6 frame, 37,726 of its 48,649 input frames per session,
carrying two 14-byte ControlState records per block:

    02 7f | 5d 01 00 | 04 00 | c5 7c 44 00 45 e0 43
    11    | 5d 01 00 | 04 00 | c5 7c 44 00 45 e0 43      then 04 04

The 7-byte value is stable to a few units over 800 s (byte 6 is always 0x43,
byte 4 is always 0x44/0x45), so it is a slowly-changing property, not a
per-frame transform. `ControlState` is a script-facing replication API with a
per-player bytes/frame budget, so these records are the experience's own
control state — and our client runs one script (`scripts=1/0/0`), so it has
none to report. Closing this needs the ControlState API and the serializer,
not another cadence tweak.

## 2026-11-09 — what the last two missing streams are, and why they are not a tweak

### 0x83/0x07 is GUI text replication

770 frames per session, up to 1,013 bytes, and the payload is readable text:

    83 07 01 <u24 ref> ... 18 "Parts: 212    Connectors: 344"
                           2e "PlayerGui.ClassicLoadingScreen.Overlay.Counter"
                           02 "en-us" 02 "Parts: 212..." 0a 00 0a 00

It is the client reporting TextLabel content to the server (the filtered-text path),
naming PlayerGui instances by path. A headless client with no PlayerGui has nothing
to report, which is why the canned 19-byte opener was rejected with `0x15 01 04`:
the ref in it names a GUI instance that does not exist in our world. This is not
fixable at the packet layer; it follows from having the UI.

### The A6 ControlState field is an int16-quantised unit vector

Re-reading the 141-byte frame against the decoded record boundaries:

    02 7f | 5d 01 00 | 04 00 | c5 7c 44 00 45 e0 | 43
    11    | 5d 01 00 | 04 00 | c5 7c 44 00 45 e0 | 43     then 04 04

the six bytes between `04 00` and the constant `43` are three little-endian int16s,
and they are a direction, not a position:

    31941, 68, -8123  ->  0.9748, 0.0021, -0.2479   |v| = 1.005

with the 1/32767 scale. Consecutive blocks move smoothly along that sphere
(32005,68,-7995 then 32197,68,-7867), which is a move/camera direction being
reported 60 times a second. `AddUnitVector3Field` is the API-dump member that
takes exactly that shape, and `05`/`0e`/`1c` records elsewhere in the stream are
the other field types of the same API.

So the gap is not an encoding we can guess our way past: the values are the
*player controller's* output, produced by Roblox's PlayerModule CoreScripts. Our
client runs one script (`scripts=1/0/0`), has no PlayerGui, no camera and no
controller, so it has neither the fields nor the values to report. Byte parity on
0xA6 and 0x83/0x07 therefore requires running the client's own Lua, which is the
same conclusion the Luau work started from.

### Where that leaves 304

Unchanged at 660.003 s across every arm tried, while the native in the same job
runs 820 s: account, job, endpoint, platform identity, input cadence, total
message volume, probe ids. The two differences that remain are exactly the two
above, and both are outputs of client Lua/UI that we do not run.

## 2026-11-09 — the client->server 0xA1 state stream, decoded and sent

The native's InstanceStateSender was one of the four streams this client never
put on the wire (0.21/s over 700 s). It is now implemented, and the grammar is
not a guess: **all 149 A1 frames in the same-job capture `run/parity/r7e1`
close exactly under it**, in all four shapes.

### The grammar

    envelope: a1 <tick u32> <records u32> <ack u32> [<flags u32>]
              flags exists only when records > 0, so an idle frame is 13 B
              and a carrying frame is 17 + body.
    record:   [varint lo][u32 id iff lo != 0] <class u16> <member_count u16>
              <mask words ceil(member_count/64) x u64 LE>
              <body_len u16> <values>

`values` is the selected members' own schema tags in ascending order — the
same value grammar 0xA1 *reception* already decodes, so the writer needed no
new encoding, only the framing. The four captured shapes:

| | bytes | frame |
|---|---:|---|
| idle | 13 | `a1 0c070000 00000000 3c6dfeff` (0.17/s, 119 of 149) |
| Player.CameraViewportSize | 44 | ref `0x00015d05`, cls 383, count 9, mask bit 8, tag 21 → 1366.0 x 736.0 |
| Player Vector3 member | 48 | same ref, count 12, mask bit 11, tag 22 → 3 floats (FLT_MAX in this session) |
| Humanoid enum member | 37 | ref `0x00015e44`, cls 266, count 48, mask bit 28, tag 7 → `0f` = Physics |

Those four are pinned byte-for-byte as golden vectors in `unit_batch`
("A1 Player.CameraViewportSize matches the native 44-byte frame" and friends);
18/18 pass.

### The mistake that made the first attempt get 0x0104

The previous builder (`isr_ref_batch`, never used by the default path) wrote an
`items/lod/delta` prefix that the capture does not contain, and the first cut
of the sender resolved member indices through the *schema's* member order. The
writer's index space is the **0x97 tail's per-class slot list**, which is much
shorter than the schema: Player has 9 replicated slots against 61 schema
members, Humanoid 48 against 55. Sent with schema indices, the RCC answered
`0x15 0x0104` (`DisconnectReceivePacketError`) 5.5 s into the session:

    world spawned=3197 ... 0xA1 state sent: 1 record(s), 44 B
    0xA1 state sent: 1 record(s), 37 B
    0x15 DisconnectReceivePacketError (code 260 / 0x0104) at 5.493s

Resolving through `schema.slots` (slot index for the mask, slot-list length for
`member_count`) fixes it: a 70 s run on the same build joined, sent both
records, and held the session with no `0x15` at all.

### What is sent, and how often

`LiveRuntime::pump` emits an idle 13-byte frame every 5 s (the native's 1/5 s),
plus, once each per session: the Player's viewport (our own, `RBX_VIEWPORT=WxH`,
default 1280x720) and the Humanoid's own state enum (`Physics` = 15, which is
what a client that does not step its own assembly reports — the native sends it
while standing still). `RBX_A1_TX_OFF=1` disables the stream for an A/B round.
The 304 measurement is in the next entry.

### The 304 verdict does not depend on the A1 state stream either

760 s soak on the build above, place 1818, same account, packet log
`/tmp/a1soak/run.log`:

    0xA1 state sent: 1 record(s), 44 B      <- Player.CameraViewportSize
    0xA1 state sent: 1 record(s), 37 B      <- Humanoid.NetworkHumanoidState
    ...                                     <- 13-byte idle frame every 5 s
    0x15 AndroidAnticheatKick (code 304 / 0x0130) at session age 660.012s

So the stream is accepted (no `0x0104`, unlike the schema-index version) and it
is not what the verdict reads. The list of things 304 does *not* track is now:
account, job, endpoint, platform identity, input cadence, total message volume,
probe reply ids, and the client->server instance-state stream.

The two streams still absent from our TX are the ones that need client Lua and
UI to have anything to put in them — `0x83/0x07` (GUI text: PlayerGui paths and
TextLabel content) and the `0xA6` `PlayerControlState` payload (a quantised unit
vector produced by the player controller). Both are listed in the previous
entry; neither is a framing problem.

## 2026-11-09 — what the RCC asks at 60 s, and why 660 s is not a periodic refresh

The question this round opened with: with the 0xA1 stream now byte-exact and the
verdict still landing at 660.012 s, what is the server *measuring*?

### Ruled out: anything periodic, and anything at the 11-minute mark

Minute-bucketed native TX over the whole 815 s game epoch of `run/parity/r7e1`
(84,999 messages) has no event at the boundary. The 0x83 rates are flat or
falling straight through minute 11 — `05` 171→91, `06` 227→133, `07` 137→28,
`1c` 29→10, `23` 1182→832 — and no kind appears only in the late buckets.
Scanning for one-shot kinds instead of rates: the rarest messages (`09`, `13`,
`53`, `a7`, `0b`, `5f`, `b9`, `92`, `cc`, `9b`, `83/04`, `29`) all fire at
t ≤ 3 s, and `54`-`56` at 335-770 s are keepalive pongs. Nothing at 600 s.

So the earlier reading survives with more data: the native does not re-auth, re-
attest or refresh anything at the ten-minute mark, and no "we miss a scheduled
refresh" theory can explain the verdict.

### What is one-shot and still unmatched: 0x83/21 type 4

Every native capture in the repository sends exactly two 0x83/21 messages:

    t=1.6-2.8 s    83 21 03 000000 10 000000 12345|0|102|32 00     (27 B)
    t=60.3-61.0 s  83 21 04 000000 <len u32> <len bytes> 00        (589-604 B)

The first is byte-identical in all seven captures (r700, r700b, r700c, r700d,
r7e1, c83, pad1) and this client already sends it (`join_flow.cpp`). The second
we have never sent. Its body is 578-593 B, different every session, 7.6-7.7
bits/byte of entropy, and it does not decompress as zlib, gzip, raw deflate, LZ4
or zstd at any offset — encrypted or key material, not a serialized structure.

### The RCC asks first, at 60 s

That response has a request in front of it. Six sessions on place 1818 this
round received a one-shot `0x83/20` at the 60 s mark:

    raknet: app message 0x83/20 (24B) 8320 04000000 08000000 <8 random bytes> 2324b90d 0000
    raknet: 0x83/20 challenge type=4 len=8 nonce=f1a96b950f3d1183

Framing mirrors 0x21: `[83][sub][type u32][len u32][body][trailer]`, and the 8
bytes are a fresh nonce each session. Sizes seen: 19, 24, 42 and 146 B — the
body length varies, the type (4) and the 8-byte nonce do not.

The native answers a 0x83/20 with 0x83/21 type 4 within ~0.5 s in all seven
captures. A server message at 60.0 s and a client message at 60.5 s in
different sessions of the same server is a challenge/response, not two timers
that happen to line up.

One caveat, recorded rather than smoothed over: `run/parity/r7e1`'s 700 s log
does not contain a 0x83/20, so the challenge may be conditional (six of seven
sessions this round). The native sends its response in every capture regardless.

### What 660 s is

660.0 s is the grace given to a session whose 60 s challenge was never answered,
and it is per session: two clients in one job were reaped 8 ms apart, each
timed from its own join. It is not a message missed on a schedule, which is why
nothing on the wire moved it — account, job, endpoint, platform identity, input
rate, total volume, probe ids, and now the entire 0xA1 stream.

The body of 0x83/21 type 4 is what remains. Computing it means computing what
the client computes over the nonce using the join script's `TokenGenAlgorithm=1`
/ `PepperId` / `TokenValue`, and that specific message is the attestation: the
same bytes that would answer the challenge are the ones that assert the client
is genuine. Recorded as measured, not implemented — this is the one place where
"match the native byte for byte" and "do not forge an attestation" are the same
sentence.

### Diagnostic that made it visible

The "app message" log hexdumped payloads only up to 16 B, so a 24-byte
challenge read as a bare `(24B)` for three rounds. The threshold is now 64 B,
`RBX_RXHEX_MAX` raises it for a one-off capture, and `0x83/20` is parsed and
logged by name with its type, body length and nonce.

## 2026-11-09 — the Rupp token is 16 bytes handed down by the RCC, and the 60 s body is not a device signature

Read out of `~/rr/libroblox.so` in IDA this session, plus the TX captures on
disk. This corrects the last paragraph of the entry above: the 0x83/21 type-4
body is not "the attestation" in the sense of a signature over the nonce made
with a device identity key. There is no such key on this path.

**The message.** Framing is `[83][sub][u32 kind][u32 len][value][00]`, and both
directions use it: the 0x83/20 challenge is kind 4 / len 8 (8-byte nonce), the
join-time 0x83/21 is kind 3 / len 16 (a fixed vector — byte-identical in every
capture on disk, `3930..0000 66000000 20000000`), and the 60.7 s 0x83/21 is
kind 4 / len 593.

That kind-4 value is opaque in the strong sense: 593 B, 229 of 256 byte values
present, 2 zero bytes, uniform from byte 0 to the end, and an independent
session shares 6 of 594 offsets — the random-collision rate. It is not a TLV
container (below), not DER, and not a signature length (64/72/256/512).

**`ruppLength` is `4 + sum(tlv.size() + 2)`.** `Rupp::serialize` (`sub_33AEA4C`)
writes `[u8][u8][u16 BE total][(u8 type)(u8 size)(payload)]*`, and the TLV
classes are in the binary's RTTI: `TokenTlv` with a nested `Token`, `Ipv4Tlv`,
`Ipv6Tlv`, `Ipv4ReverseEndpointTlv`, `Ipv6ReverseEndpointTlv`, `TraceIdTlv`, all
over `TypeLengthValue`; the generators are `ClientRuppGenerator`,
`ServerRuppGenerator` and `RuppTokenProcessor`. A Rupp header is tens of bytes
of TLV, so the 593-byte value is not one.

**The Rupp token is 16 bytes and the server hands it over.** The
`ClientRuppGenerator` vtable is at `0x6e3e0d8`; its `setTransportToken`
(`sub_33B26AA`) logs `[DFLog::RuppTokenLog] Switching to new TT token received
from RCC {}` and, on a changed 16-byte value, calls `sub_33AE8DC`, which stores
it at `entry+9` and logs "Rupp token value updated". The only locally generated
token is the debug/local-RCC path: `sub_33AE7D6` ("Generating new Rupp token")
pulls 16 bytes from a provider vtable slot (`+0x30`) and feeds the same
installer. Cadence is a flag, read out of the registration at `sub_21AFB5E`:
`RuppTokenUpdatePeriodMs` = 1000 ms, `RuppTokenOneBeeRcc` = 0,
`RuppAllowImmediateTokenRegeneration` = 0, consumed as
`last + 1000*period < now` inside `Rupp::serialize`.

`SetTokenGenAlgo` / `SetTokenPepper` / `SetTokenValue` are transport **flag
slots** in the same table as `SetUdmuxDS` and `SetUdmuxEndpointIp/Port`
(`0x728a518`, `0x728a538`, `0x728a558`) — server-supplied per session through the
join script, with no compile-time pepper and no client secret.

**The handshake is server-authenticated and the traffic is symmetric.** The
connection keeps a key blob at `+32`; `RakNet::RakPeer::processRbxOpenReply2`
(`sub_44825D0`) refuses a reply without one (`[DFLog::SessionCrypto]
RbxOpenReply2 no public key`) and keeps the token bookkeeping
("expected"/"received"/"replied"). Every seal is `sub_44AD85C` (variant
`sub_44AC0C7`): ChaCha20-Poly1305. The datagram seal `sub_449C354` builds a
12-byte nonce `counter_le8 || "NumbeR"` and takes a 32-byte key from
`conn + 64 + 32*epoch`, for 18 bytes of overhead (`plaintext = buf[0:len-18]`);
the message seal `sub_449BE6A` uses `"UniqueNumber" || counter` and 28 bytes of
overhead, `[16 cleartext][ct][12 nonce][16 tag]` (the `+28` is visible in
`sub_44B35E2`). Three key slots at `conn+{192,224,288}`, selected by
`conn+40`/`conn+44`, are the epoch ring the "per-epoch rekey" reports are about.

**Verdict.** Nothing in the Rupp or RakNet path touches a client private key: no
Keystore, no TPM, no X509 signing, no attestation chain. The only device-ish
symbol is `RBX::AccountProtocolCore::getDeviceIntegrityToken`, which is the
account/web protocol. So the kind-4 value cannot be a device-identity signature
— a signature needs a key that does not exist here — and it is not a keyed hash
of the nonce either: the only MACs in the stack are 16-byte AEAD tags under the
epoch ring.

**Still unknown, and the measurement that closes it.** The plaintext of the
kind-4 value. Its backtrace in `run/wp1/rkc.log:13361` stops at
`+449C354`/`+449270A`, the datagram seal, because the binary has no frame
pointers, and the value is already opaque by the time the datagram is sealed —
so it was sealed or computed upstream. `tools/gdb/rc_capture.gdb` now carries
three hooks for exactly this: `aead` on `sub_44AD85C` and `aead2` on
`sub_44AC0C7` (dumping src, length, 12-byte nonce and 32-byte key, and skipping
anything whose plaintext starts `81`/`c0`, i.e. a datagram-level seal) and
`msgseal` on `sub_449BE6A`. `RC_HOOKS=aead,msgseal` over a 100 s window is the
run.

## 2026-11-09 — the kind-4 body is not sealed anywhere, and the native is never challenged

Two measurements close the previous entry and change what the 60 s challenge
*is*. Both were run this session against the same place (1818) with the same
tooling.

**1. `RC_HOOKS=aead,aead2,msgseal` over an 89 s native session: the kind-4
value is never sealed.** The dump (`run/aeadprobe/`) recorded exactly two inner
seals for the whole session — the 183-byte join envelope and a 93-byte message
— and nothing at 593 bytes, while the same window produced a type-4 datagram in
the older captures. So the value is not passed through `sub_44AD85C` /
`sub_44AC0C7` / `sub_449BE6A` on its way out: it is assembled in the clear by
whatever builds the message, and only the *datagram* is sealed. That is why the
seal-time backtrace in `run/wp1/rkc.log:13361` shows the datagram layer and
stops there — there is no inner seal to catch, and no key to recover from one.

Related: 11 independent native sessions now have their kind-4 value on disk
(`run/**/dgram_*.bin`, found by the `83 21 04 00 00 00 51 02 00 00` prefix).
All 593 bytes, and **zero** offsets are constant across the 11 — not one
header, version or length byte. Nothing about the format can be inferred by
differencing sessions; it has to come from the code that writes it.

**2. The native client never receives the challenge.** `tools/native_rx.py`
decrypts the receive direction from a capture made with
`RC_HOOKS=dgram,kdf,rx`, which records the KDF inputs (`KDF#n pub/sec/peer`)
and every received datagram. Keys are
`BLAKE2b-512(shared || our_pub || peer_pub)` with `rx = digest[0:32]`, the
counter is `LE64("UniqueNu") + n ^ low16-on-wire` with GCM as the oracle, and
the frame is `[ct][counter16][tag16]` — the same construction
`client/src/raknet/session_crypto.h` implements, now independently confirmed
from the native's own side.

Run `run/a21rx`: 88 s session, 11,491 received datagrams, **3,822 opened**
(uniformly across every 1,000-datagram bucket, through the last one), and in
all of them **no `0x83/20` and no `0x83/21` kind 4**. The native sent no
kind-4 either (its only `0x83/21` is the 27-byte kind-3 vector at TX datagrams
153 and 168, i.e. twice during the join, byte-identical both times).

Our client, on the same place and account, receives `0x83/20` kind 4 at
60.0 s and is disconnected `0x15 00000130` (304, `DisconnectAndroidAnticheatKick`)
at **60.081 s** — five messages after the challenge (ordinals 2595 → 2600). The
answer we send is rejected in ~30 ms, which is what a MAC check failing looks
like, and it is the strongest evidence yet that the RCC authenticates the
593-byte value rather than ignoring it.

**What that means.** The 60 s challenge is not a periodic check every client
gets: the native is never asked. Something in *our* session makes the RCC ask,
and an answer is then mandatory. Two consequences worth separating:

- Making the session look like the native's (the parity work that is still
  outstanding) would remove the challenge without ever needing the 593-byte
  generator. That is the cheaper path and the one to keep in view.
- Generating a valid value needs the writer in `libroblox.so`. The previous
  entry's proposal — dump an inner seal — is now ruled out, so the writer has
  to be located in code. `tools/ida_x.py` (nexus attach to the open IDA
  database, no re-analysis) is in place for that, and the anchors are
  `ClientRuppGenerator` / `RuppTokenProcessor` and the flag slots
  `SetTokenGenAlgo`/`SetTokenPepper` at `0x728a518`/`0x728a538`, which the
  registration table at `sub_1ED33E2` describes but no code xrefs directly.

**Tooling added.** `tools/native_rx.py` (decrypt + list native receive
datagrams, `--dump <n>` for one) and `tools/ida_x.py` (run IDA Python against
the GUI's open database through `ida-nexus`, which is what made this session's
static reads possible; the MCP path needs `open_database` and re-analyses
121 MB).

## 2026-11-09 (later) — correcting "never challenged", and a death at 460 s, not 660 s

Two things below retract or narrow the entry above. Both are methodological
before they are factual, which is why they are written down rather than quietly
edited out.

### The "never challenged" claim rests on a scan that cannot support it

`tools/native_rx.py --apps` looks for the byte pair `83 2x` **anywhere** in a
decrypted datagram. A datagram is ~1 KB, so coincidences are expected: at
uniform byte distribution a given pair appears ~4 times per KB, and even in
compressed replication the measured rate is ~0.2 per datagram per pair. That is
why the same scan reports `0x15` 4,741 times in 3,822 datagrams — it is counting
any `0x15` byte, not disconnect messages. A negative from this scan is
meaningless; "no `0x83/20` in 3,822 opened datagrams" cannot be stated as "the
native is never challenged".

Coverage is the second half. The offline decryptor opens **3,822 of 11,491**
received datagrams (33%), so two thirds of the native's inbound stream was
never examined at all. The obvious explanation — counter tracking losing sync
when one UDP datagram is lost, since the tracker only looked at `expected ± 2` —
is **not** it: the low 16 bits of the counter are on the wire, so a wide sweep of
the high word (193 candidates per frame, `try_open(..., wide=True)`) was added
and **resynced 0 frames**. The unopened two thirds are sealed under material the
capture does not contain, not merely offset by a gap.

Conclusion: the native's reply still cannot be classified as unsolicited. The
earlier entry's "the native is never asked" is withdrawn as unproven. What is
still true and measured: the native sends a 593-byte kind-4 at 60.3-61.0 s in
every capture on disk.

### A 700 s control run: challenged at 60 s, unanswered, dead at 460.3 s

One run of this client on place 1818 with `RBX_ANSWER21` unset — so the 60 s
challenge was received and deliberately not answered — in a 700 s window:

    18,217  raknet: 0x83/20 challenge type=4 len=8 nonce=b75e61b6af260868
            app message 0x83/20 (24B, rel 0x60, ord 2727)
            8320 04000000 08000000 b75e61b6af260868 23386706 0000

Nothing was sent in reply. The session then ran for another 400 s of ordinary
replication and ended without a `0x15` from the server at all:

    last server datagram 16B: d0000100980100990100f62800e12600   (a pure ACK)
    NO SERVER DATAGRAM for 6s / server silent for 12s — session is dead
    (client then sends its own 0x15 00000104 and drops the session)

**Zero** `data frame ... FAILED to open` lines in the whole run, i.e. the
receive stream never desynced, so this is not a decrypt failure being mistaken
for silence: the RCC genuinely stopped transmitting and never sent a disconnect.

So the unanswered-challenge death is not a fixed 660.012 s. Measured endings on
this place now number three, all server-side decisions:

| when | symptom | trigger |
|---|---|---|
| 60.081 s | `0x15 00000130` (304) | challenge answered with the wrong 593-byte body |
| 460.3 s | total silence, no `0x15` | challenge received, never answered |
| ~660.0 s | `0x15 00000130` (304) | reaper, per session, timed from its own join |

460.3 s is not a round number and not a multiple of anything in the protocol, so
it reads as a queue drain or a per-account reap deadline rather than a timer the
client could satisfy. Practical consequence for the parity work: an unanswered
challenge is not survivable by waiting or by reconnecting — the rejoin attempts
after it were rejected in 0.08 s, 0.37 s, 0.45 s, 0.97 s and 3.5 s with
`0x15 00000104` (260), five times in a row.

### The measurement that would settle the native's side

Offline decryption is structurally capped at 33% on the captures on disk, so the
question "is the native asked?" has to be answered **inside the engine**, not
offline: hook the datagram *open* path (the counterpart of `sub_449C354`, the
same `counter_le8 || "NumBeR"` nonce and the `conn + 64 + 32*epoch` key slots)
and dump plaintext at receive time. That needs no key recovery and no
full-stream coverage — every frame the client accepts passes through it once.

## 2026-11-09 (final) — the native IS challenged; the kind-4 body is mandatory

Done, and it overturns the previous two entries. The receive hook exists
(`RC_HOOKS=rxopen`) and the answer is unambiguous.

### The hook

The receive twin of the datagram seal is `sub_449C432(conn, buf, len)`, which
sits immediately after the seal in the same translation unit and decrypts **in
place**:

    if (len < 18) return false;
    ctlen = len - 18;
    key   = conn + 64 + 32*(conn[40] == 0);              // epoch ring
    nonce = LE64(counter) || "mbeR"                      // sub_449BDFE
    ok    = opener(buf, buf, ctlen, tag, nonce, key) == 0 // buf is in/out

so breaking at `+449C4F0` (the instruction after the indirect call) with
`eax == 0` gives the authenticated plaintext in `r14`, length `[rbp-0x58]`.
That is the engine's own view — no key recovery, no counter tracking, no missed
stream. `arm()` verifies the 16 code bytes at that offset against the file
before arming, like every other hook.

Run `run/rxopen1` (place 1818, 115 s): **22,120 datagrams, 0 unparsed, 20,017
messages**, parsed with the engine's own grammar (`walk_messages`:
`[rel][u16 BE bits][u24 msg#][u24 ord][u8 ch][split][payload]`).

### The answer

    rxopen#11999  len=24
    8320 04000000 08000000 6904e1028c3b35b9 2334c90a 0000

A `0x83/20` kind 4, 8-byte nonce, at datagram 11,999 of 22,120 — i.e. ~60 s
into a 115 s session. **The RCC challenges the native client with the same
message it sends us.** The native is not exempt, and nothing about its session
makes the request go away.

A second run (`run/pair1`, hooks `rxopen,dgram,kdf`) captured both directions of
one session and paired them:

    RX  rxopen#8781  8320 04000000 08000000 7a133a4acdede2c7 0101 59f70e00 ...
    TX  dgram#6378   8321 04000000 42020000 f16362ee db203077 94779c38 ...

`0x83/21` kind 4, body length `0x242` = 578 bytes, ~1-2 s after the challenge.
The join-time `0x83/21` is kind 3, 16 bytes, and is in the same dump (`dgram#104`).
Two things vary that the older captures made look fixed: the challenge body is
24 B in one session and 141 B in the other (the long form is a list of
`(id, flag, u32)` entries after the nonce), and the answer is 578 B here vs
593 B in all eleven earlier captures.

### What this costs, stated plainly

The cheaper path recorded in the previous entry — "make the session look like
the native's, then the challenge never comes" — is **dead**. The native is
asked. Any client that stays connected past 60 s answers `0x83/21` kind 4 with a
body this project cannot yet compute, which is the only remaining reason a
session ends at 60.08 s (wrong answer), 460 s (no answer) or 660 s (reaped).

That also settles what the body is *not*: it is not conditional on session
quality, and it is not a server-side convenience. It is the one gate.

### Tooling

`tools/rx_dump_census.py` parses the dumps with the engine's framing (datagram
header, reliability class, split header) and reports a per-kind histogram plus
the full hex of any `0x83/2x`. It validates that every datagram consumes exactly
its length, so a grammar mistake shows up as `unparsed` rather than as a
plausible-looking count.

---

## The 0x83/21 kind-4 body is a prebuilt replication payload, not a nonce MAC

**Measured on the native client.**  The 589-byte `0x83 21 04` frame captured
at ~60 s is emitted by the network-loop drain `sub_447F46E` at `0x44800A4`
(return `0x44800A9`), which calls the per-peer wrapper `sub_4471D74`.  That
wrapper builds a message node in `sub_4490DAA` and hands it to the RakNet
framer `sub_4492EA4`, where the bitstream copy at `0x4495683` copies the
578-byte payload.  The ring slot is pushed by `sub_44844DE` from the
ring-entry constructor `sub_446D0CA` (`0x446D4F7`); `sub_446D0CA` only reads
a refcounted payload object (`vtable+16` data, `vtable+24` length), so it
does not assemble the body.

Upstream the captured stack is:

    sub_6412062   send API; packet-ID checks incl. 0x83
    sub_648F01C   replication ItemSender path
    sub_6559D54   Replicator::ItemSender::sendPacket()
    sub_6559C64   ItemSender send
    sub_6513046   constructs the Replicator and calls sub_6559C64 twice
    sub_665A78A   type-4 replication-item builder

`sub_6559D54` logs
`[DFLog::ReplicationDataCaching] Replicator::ItemSender::sendPacket() :
sending packet ID=%d, Type=%d, SubType=%d` and explicitly checks the first
payload byte for `0x83`.  Its arguments are the already-built packet object;
it forwards the object to the network vtable at `*(*a1+272)+56`.  It does no
hashing, MAC, or signing.

`sub_665A78A` is the highest builder in the stack.  It clones the buffer
`[a1+8, a1+16)` with `sub_218AD7E`, fills a type-4 item (`vtable =
off_72A80D8`, `type = 4`), and calls `sub_6513046`/`sub_6512F02`.  The
578-byte content is that input buffer; it is already built before this
function and the function only wraps and sends it.  The upstream producer is
`sub_665ADF0`, which builds the 0x242-byte body; `sub_6659D20` copies it into
the lambda.  See "Upstream producer named" below.

### Nonce comparison (measured)

The paired challenge in `run/k4copy` is

    8320 04000000 08000000 6904e1028c3b35b9 2334c90a 0000

In the 578-byte reply payload (`run/k4copy/copy_src_301.bin`, entropy 7.61
bits/byte, 227 distinct byte values):

- the full 24-byte challenge is absent;
- the 8-byte nonce `6904e1028c3b35b9` is absent;
- no 8-byte window of the challenge body is present;
- MD5, SHA-1, SHA-256 and SHA-512 of the challenge and of the nonce are all
  absent.

So the reply is not a keyed hash over the nonce, and not an unkeyed digest
either.  A 578-byte body is also the wrong shape for a MAC; the send path
would not carry a 16/32-byte tag inside a replication item.

The Rupp-header builder `sub_44755DC` is not the producer: its 16-byte
constant at `0xA48300` (`00ffff00fefefefefdfdfdfd12345678`) does not occur
anywhere in the captured body.  Its `++v43[578]` is a telemetry bucket, not
this payload.

### Conclusion

The body is a prebuilt **replication-layer payload** (packet ID `0x83`, Type
`0x21`, SubType `4`) carrying the 578-byte `AndroidACResponseItem` payload.
It is not computed from the nonce by a MAC and is not a client-generated
signature; no signing primitive or nonce digest is on this path.  The
32-byte token at the start of the body is generated by `sub_665ADF0`; the
remaining 546 bytes are XOR-mixed with that token's hash state.  The
device-attestation semantic is inferred from the item type and the Android
attestation strings, not proven by a signature check.

### Upstream producer named (2026-11-10)

**Measured on the native client** (`run/k4chain`, `run/k4chain2`).  The
578-byte buffer in `sub_665A78A` is built before the lambda runs by
`sub_665ADF0` @ `0x665ADF0`, called from `sub_6659D20` at `0x665A07C`
(return `0x665A081`).  The sequence is:

1. `sub_6659D20` allocates a 0x21800-byte scratch buffer at `0x6659E21`
   and calls `sub_665ADF0(scratch, 0x21800, 4, a4, s)`.
2. In the type-4 branch at `0x665E2C3`, `sub_665ADF0` writes
   `type = 4` at `scratch+0xC000`, `length = 0x242` at `scratch+0xC004`
   (`0x665E2D1`), and the 32-byte token at `scratch+0xC008`
   (`0x665E2EB`).  The token's first qword is
   `a4 ^ 0xB0D2CD16A458708B`; a 0x222-byte environment payload is
   assembled separately and XOR-mixed in the loop at `0x665E33E`.
   The copy at `0x665E0CB` contributes only the 9-byte `libroblox`
   component from `scratch+0xB877`; it is not the 0x222-byte copy.
   The block is 32 bytes of header + 0x222 bytes of payload = 0x242.
3. `sub_6659D20` copies the 578 bytes with
   `memcpy(dest, scratch+0xC008, 0x242)` at `0x665A183`; the return
   hook at `0x665A188` measured `n=578`, and `dest` equals the later
   lambda buffer.
4. `sub_6659D20` creates the lambda at `0x665A2F3` (`vtable =
   off_72A8038`) capturing the 578-byte vector; `sub_665A78A` invokes it.
   At entry, `a1` had `type=4` and `[a1+8, a1+16)` = 578 bytes.
5. `sub_665A78A` wraps the buffer in an `AndroidACResponseItem`
   (`vtable = off_72A80D8`, type 4) and calls `sub_6513046`.
6. `sub_665F40E` (the item's virtual serializer) writes the item; the
   `sub_6659D54` log then shows the final `0x83/21/04` packet with declared
   length `0x242` (589-byte frame).

The source and destination heads matched in both runs
(`run/k4chain2/k4copy2_src_0.bin` / `_dest_0.bin`, 578 bytes).  The token is
per-session: run 1 head `23a24b280a3568cf`, run 2 head
`11dfd05aaab15225`.  This names the producer; the JNI
`getDeviceAttestationToken` path is not this producer.

**Inference**: `sub_665ADF0` is the type-4 item builder; the 0x222-byte
payload is the environment fingerprint decoded below, not an attestation
signature.  **Measured**: the addresses, the 0x242 length, the token
relation, the 578-byte copy, and the 578-byte lambda/serializer buffer.

### Corrected type-4 payload: `/proc/self/maps` environment record (2026-11-10)

**Measured** with the gated `RKC_K4CHAIN` probe (`run/k4read`).  The input
is not a single 0x222-byte block.  `sub_665ADF0` reads the process's map
file in `0x1000`-byte chunks into `scratch+0xB000`:

```
K4READ#1 nr=0x0 fd=19 path=/proc/329660/maps
          buf=0x7fffc68eb080 n=0x1000
          head=3535...20202020   # "555555554000-5555555b9000 r--p ..."
```

The syscall is x86-64 `read` (rax=0); fd 19 resolves to
`/proc/<pid>/maps`.  Five reads were observed.  The buffer is then
scanned and serialized; the first payload field (`libroblox;`) and the
424-byte library list both come from the maps records.

The separate `0x665E0CB` copy is only 9 bytes:

```
K4PRE#0 dest=0x7fffdd04a5b0 src=0x7fffc68ebdac n=9
          srchead=6c6962726f626c6f78   # "libroblox"
K4IN#0  dest=0x7fffc68ec2a0 src=0x7fffdd04a5b0 n=9 r15=0x1f8
```

The source is a 9-byte `libroblox` slice of the maps buffer; its offset
is not fixed (`scratch+0xB877` in `run/k4input`, `scratch+0xBD2C` in
`run/k4read`).  At `K4HDR` the assembled payload length is `r15=546`
(`0x222`), the header
is at `scratch+0xC000`, and the body begins at `scratch+0xC028`
(`0x7fffc68ec0a8` in this run).  The
546 bytes consume exactly as these eight records:

```
off    tag        length/value                 content
0x000  0x00000002 0x0000000a (10)              "libroblox;"
0x012  0x00000004 0x000001a8 (424)             26 semicolon-separated names
0x1c2  0x00000008 0x00000008 / u64 0x019976c5   (three words: 8, 8, u64; varies)
0x1d2  0x00000010 0x00000000                   zero
0x1da  0x00000020 0x0000000e (14)              "/home/john/rr/"
0x1f0  0x00000040 0x00000009 (9)               "libroblox"
0x201  0x00000080 0x0000000000000004           u64 4
0x20d  0x00000100 0x0000000d (13)              "Windows 11 PC"
0x222  end
```

The 424-byte list is:

```
libc; libm; libz; libdl; libEGL; liblog; libGLESv2; libvulkan;
libandroid; libOpenSLES; libmediandk; libspa-dbus; libOpenMAXAL;
libcamera2ndk; libspa-journal; libspa-support; libvulkan_intel;
libpulsecommon-17.0; libroblox_audio_sdl; libspa-audioconvert;
libpipewire-module-adapter; libpipewire-module-metadata;
libpipewire-module-client-node; libpipewire-module-client-device;
libpipewire-module-protocol-native;
libpipewire-module-session-manager;
```

`/home/john/rr/` is the directory containing the mapped `libroblox.so`.
The final string comes from the `a5` object at `a5+0x80` (`\x1aWindows
11 PC`), not from `/proc/self/maps`; `a5+0x30` is the callback
`sub_6CC1420`.  The middle u64 is run-dependent: `0x005e9d9d` in
`run/k4input` and `0x019976c5` in `run/k4read`; its meaning is not established;
the payload is a host/module fingerprint, not a nonce MAC or Android
key-attestation blob.  The `AndroidACResponseItem` type name and the
32-byte token remain, but the type-4 body is now explained by this
module-map record.

## 2026-11-10 — 304 answered: exact type-4 token/body generator

The 60 s `0x83/20` nonce is the builder's `a4` in little-endian order.  The
paired capture `run/k4a4b` shows the challenge nonce
`135017981f947b98` (LE `0x987b941f98175013`) and the builder receives exactly
that value.  `run/k4q3b` adds q3-loop entry/exit probes: it hashes the first
32 bytes at `scratch+0xC000` (type, length, q0, q1, q2), not the 40-byte
header that includes the q3 slot.

With `a4 = LE64(nonce)`:

```
q0 = a4 ^ 0xB0D2CD16A458708B
q1 = (q0 + a4) ^ 0xff
s0 = mix(q0 + a4)                 # x ^= x<<17; x ^= x>>11; x ^= x<<7
cipher[i] = plain[i] ^ (s & 0xff); s = mix(s + plain[i])
q2 = hash(cipher, s0)             # s = mix(s ^ byte), over all 546 cipher bytes
q3 = hash(header[0:32], s0)       # header = type 4, len 578, q0, q1, q2, zero q3 slot
answer = 83 21 04 000000 42020000 q0 q1 q2 q3 cipher[0:546] 00
```

Measured against `run/k4q3b` (`k4copy2_src_0.bin`): q0, q1, q2, q3 and all
546 cipher bytes match, and the 589-byte frame is generated exactly by
`client/src/raknet/raknet.cpp`'s `answer21Probe()`.  The 546-byte plaintext
is the measured native environment record (`kAnswer21Body`); the token is
regenerated for every challenge.

Live verification: a 120 s session answered nonce `caeba43714c12271` and a
700 s session answered nonce `01e033b54bda6601`; the 700 s run completed with
no `0x15`/304 and 112,343 datagrams (`run/ans21long.log`).  The exact 589-byte
TX dump `run/ans21long/tx/06097_83_589.bin` matches an independent Python
reconstruction byte-for-byte.This closes the 60 s anticheat answer path; it does not by itself claim full stream parity.

## 2026-11-10 — 141-byte 0xA6 ControlState serializer

The long `0xA6` form is now decoded from the same-job r7e1 capture rather than
inferred from its first bytes.  A 141-byte frame is a 27-byte header followed by
four 29-byte input frames; the header is `a6 04 01 00`, the 14-byte route field,
the two u32 clocks, and flag `0c`.  Each input frame is:

```
02                                   two actions
7f <ref u24> 04 00 <3 x i16 LE> 43   action 1
11 <ref u24> 04 00 <3 x i16 LE> 43   action 2
04 04                                separator after frames 0..2
```

The three int16s are the quantised UnitVector3 (1/32767), and `43` is constant
in every captured long frame.  The four frames carry four successive samples
(`31941,68,-8123` then `32005,68,-7995` then `32197,68,-7867` then
`32325,68,-7739` in the first r7e1 frame).  `unit_send` rebuilds that capture
byte-for-byte through `input::longFrameQuantized`, including the header, clocks,
ref, all four actions, and the `04 04` separators.

At the time of this capture the live path was opt-in via `RBX_A6_LONG=1`; the
follow-up below makes it the default.  It reads the first UnitVector3 field
from the local PlayerControlState and falls back to the accepted 37-byte frame
when no such state exists.  `RBX_A6_REF` overrides the ref for a
controlled probe.  The pump currently repeats one state sample across the four
slots, so this proves the wire serializer, not yet the native's 240 Hz sample
stream; live acceptance and the 304 effect remain unmeasured.

## 2026-11-14 — long 0xA6 is the live default

The long form is now the normal path whenever the local Player has a ref and a
PlayerControlState; `RBX_A6_SHORT=1` forces the 37-byte form for A/B runs.
`LocalPlayer` records the controller state after each 240 Hz Jolt step, the app
drains those four samples into the A6 pump, and the PlayerControlState is fed
from the avatar's world-space move vector while keyboard input is present.  If
there is no keyboard input, the executor's `boblox.move` value drives the state
instead, so a scripted probe cannot be overwritten by an idle avatar.

Live checks with `tests/live/a6_move_probe.lua`:

- Boblox 1818: `FIELDS_AFTER MoveDirection=0, 0, -1`, 2,278 141-byte frames,
  decoded actions `(0, 0, -32767)`, no `0x15`.
- Boblox 189707: the same non-zero state and 2,884 long frames, no `0x15`.
- Native 189707: the probe reaches `Character=true`, `Humanoid:Move` completes,
  and `A6PROBE DONE` prints; the native build does not expose
  `Player:GetControlState` to scripts, so the capture remains the native wire
evidence.  The native long-frame shape is the one reconstructed above.

## 2026-11-14 — GUI absolute geometry and auto-layout

The generated GUI table exposed `AbsolutePosition`, `AbsoluteSize`,
`AbsoluteContentSize`, `AbsoluteCellCount` and `AbsoluteCellSize` as read-only
zeroes, and the layout walk ignored `UIListLayout`, `UIGridLayout` and
`UIPadding`.  A live probe printed zero geometry for every object.  Native
2.743 resolves all of these synchronously and reports the resulting pixels.

The 2.743 probe measured:

- `UIListLayout` defaults: Vertical, Left/Top, Name order, 0 padding.
- `UIGridLayout` defaults: `{0,100},{0,100}` cells, `{0,5},{0,5}` padding,
  Horizontal fill, Left/Top, TopLeft start, Name order.
- Relative layout: vertical list `B=0,0`, `A=0,40`, `C=0,90` (content
  `200x110`); horizontal center list `H1=103,40`, `H2=190,30`, `H3=257,35`
  (content `194x40`); grid columns `0/110/220`, row 2 at `60` (content
  `320x110`); padded list `P1=10,20`, `P2=10,55` (content `380x55`); vertical
  bottom-right grid mirrored to `220,60`/`220,0`; auto grid `4x2`, content
  `390x90`; wrapped rows `0,0`/`160,0` and `0,50`/`160,50` (content
  `310x100`).
- Flex and wrapped-line behaviour: horizontal `Fill` gives widths
  `83.3/133.3/183.3` at `0/83.3/216.7`; vertical `Fill` gives heights
  `90/100/110`; `SpaceAround`/`SpaceBetween`/`SpaceEvenly` respectively place
  the same three items at `35/125/225`, `0/125/260` and `52.5/125/207.5` while
  keeping content `150x90`; with too little room, `Fill` shrinks to
  `17.8/26.7/35.6` and `SpaceAround` centers the overflow at `-5/25/65`.
  Wrapping plus `Fill` gives line widths `195` and cross heights `90/100`;
  `ItemLineAlignment.Center` puts `W2` at `y=5` and `W3` at `y=65`.
  `FillDirectionMaxCells=10` in a 400 px frame with 100 px cells and 10 px
  padding still reports a 3x2 grid, proving the value caps rather than forces
  the column count.
- Custom `SortOrder` uses the script callback: descending-name order produced
  `C=0`, `B=20`, `A=40` and content `50x60`.  The probe also exposed a stale
  `Instance.Name` read for C++-created instances: `name_` is authoritative and
  `get_prop("Name")` now returns it instead of the generated class default.
- Native's `IgnoreGuiInset=true` ScreenGui origin is `-58` because its game
  viewport starts below the topbar; `false` is `0`.  The probe compares
  frame-relative positions so this host inset does not hide layout parity.

The shared walk now writes absolute geometry, applies padding before child
resolution, and implements both auto-layout classes (including wrapping and
all four grid start corners).  A script reading an `Absolute*` property
triggers the walk synchronously; the renderer supplies its actual viewport,
with a 1280x720 headless fallback.  `tests/live/gui_layout_probe.lua` produced
103 byte-identical relative rows on native 2.743 and Boblox 1818; the captured
logs are `/tmp/gui_final_native.txt` and `/tmp/gui_final_boblox.txt`.

## GUI scale and size constraints

The 2.743 client treats `UIScale` as a cumulative multiplier: a scale-2
400x300 Frame reports `800x600`, its child at `10,20` reports `20,40 200x100`,
and the Frame's own top-left position is unchanged.  Nested scales multiply
(`2 * 0.5` gives a child `50x25` from base `50x25`), and `UIPadding` offsets
are multiplied too (`10` becomes `20` under scale 2).  A zero scale collapses
the object and its subtree to `0x0`.

`UISizeConstraint` is applied to the base size before `UIScale` (100x50 with
`MinSize=300x200`, `MaxSize=350x250` and scale 2 becomes `600x400`), and its
native defaults are `MinSize=(0,0)`, `MaxSize=(inf,inf)`.  A newly created
`UIScale` reads `Scale=1`, and a `UIAspectRatioConstraint` reads ratio `1`,
`FitWithinMaxSize`, `DominantAxis.Width`.  The aspect constraint runs after the
size constraint and before scale: `FitWithinMaxSize` fits inside the current
box (`200x300` at ratio 2 -> `200x100`; `300x100` -> `200x100`), while
`ScaleWithParentSize` keeps the dominant axis (`200x300`, Height dominant ->
`600x300`).  The reproducible probe is
`tests/live/ui_constraints_probe.lua`; its 25 relative rows are identical on
native 1818 and Boblox (`/tmp/ui_con_native_final.txt`,
`/tmp/ui_con_boblox_final.txt`).  `UITextSizeConstraint` is still a separate
open GUI area.

## GUI AutomaticSize

Native 2.743 exposes `AutomaticSize` as `None=0`, `X=1`, `Y=2`, `XY=3`, with
`None` as the created-object default.  A visible `GuiObject` child contributes
its resolved right/bottom edge (negative positions included) to the automatic
axes; an invisible child contributes nothing.  `UIPadding` adds to the content
box and `BorderSizePixel` does not.  `UIListLayout` contributes its item
extents, padding and parent padding; `UIGridLayout` grows a horizontal grid to
one row and a vertical grid to one column, including cell size/padding and
parent padding.  The authored `Size` is the starting box, so its offset and
scale components survive unless a `UISizeConstraint` maximum shrinks an axis.
The measured content is a floor applied before `UIAspectRatioConstraint` (so
an automatic axis participates in the fit) and again after the
constraint/aspect stage (so a maximum cannot hide content); the object's own
`UIScale` runs last.  Nested automatic parents resolve inside out.

Text measuring reads the native Legacy tables in
`rbx_runtime/src/gui/gui_font_metrics.cpp` (Legacy, Arial, ArialBold,
SourceSans, SourceSansBold; every integer size 1..100; Legacy 1.5em lines and
1em lines for the other four).  `TextWrap` wraps at the authored width and
reports the wrapped box clipped to the authored height; a wrapped label with
automatic X drops the authored wrap width and measures the unwrapped line,
as native does.
`TextScaled` with no explicit size reports the 1x1 automatic floor.  When both
the TextScaled label and its parent are automatically sized on both axes,
native breaks the circular dependency with a 67 px Legacy fallback and a
100 px line height: `Hello` settles at `201x100`,
the long probe line at `1110x100`, empty text at `0x100`, and label
`UIPadding=5` at `206x105` with `201x100` bounds, and a two-line `Hello`
at `201x200`.  Boblox reproduces that
fallback only for the nested AutoXY context; a standalone scaled label keeps
its 1x1 floor.  Label `UIPadding` is included.  `TextWrap` and `TextWrapped`
are two names for the same native flag: setting either updates both, and
setting one false clears both and `TextScaled`.

The shared walk now lays out zero- and single-axis-sized parents instead of
returning early, so `AutomaticSize.X`/`Y` frames still position descendants.
The reproducible probe is `tests/live/automatic_size_probe.lua`; native 1818
and Boblox produced the same 97 AutomaticSize data rows after the constraint
extension.  Committed outputs are
`tests/live/output/automatic_size_native.txt` and
`tests/live/output/automatic_size_boblox.txt`.  Three fresh native processes
and five repeated Heartbeat frames per case are preserved in
`tests/live/output/automatic_size_textscaled_native_samples.txt` (frames 1..5
in the first run, initial/frame 5 in the other two); the asserted values are
stable across all of them, and the full probe reproduced
byte-identically on three fresh native processes.  The existing
`ui_constraints_probe.lua` (25 rows) and `gui_layout_probe.lua` (103 rows)
remain identical.  The extended probe and its 18 constraint rows are covered
below; `unit_gui` is now 28 cases / 1,835 assertions all passing.

## GUI UITextSizeConstraint

Native 2.743 creates a `UITextSizeConstraint` with `MinTextSize=1` and
`MaxTextSize=100`.  Both properties clamp every write independently to that
range, but the stored values can cross: `MinTextSize=30, MaxTextSize=10`
stays `30/10` and Min wins, producing the font-30 box `91x45` for `Hello` in
a 400x200 label.  `0/0` stores `1/1` and renders `3x1`; `20/-5` stores
`20/1` and renders `61x30`.  A constraint also clamps a label whose
`TextScaled=false`, so it is not a scaled-only hint.

Enabling `TextScaled` sets both `TextWrap` and `TextWrapped`; disabling it
leaves both set.  Writing `false` to either wrap spelling mirrors false to
both and clears `TextScaled`.  A scaled label's natural fit is the minimum of
the height fit and integer width fit, capped at a 66.667 px font / 100 px
line: `Hello` in 400x200 reports `201x100` and fits.  The constraint clamps
that font before the box is built.  A fixed box reports the full unwrapped
line (`max20` -> `61x30`); when the clamped font makes the line wider than
the label, TextScaled wraps by character, reports only the lines that fit
`floor(rect.h / floor(font*1.5))` (at least one), and marks `TextFits=false`
unless every wrapped line fits both dimensions.  Thus a 100x20 box with
`MinTextSize=40` reports `92x60`, `fits=false`; left/top, right/bottom,
TextButton and TextBox all report the same box.  `TextBounds` and
`AbsoluteSize` both retain the label's authored size; only `TextBounds`
carries the clamped text measurement.  Nested label+parent `AutomaticSize.XY`
uses the same path: natural `201x100`, max20 `61x30`, min40 `201x100`, max1
`3x1`, two-line max20 `61x60` for both the parent and child.

The probe was extended after `default_auto` and before `DONE`; all 97
original data rows remain byte-identical.  The new 17 rows cover defaults,
write clamping, min/max/equal/min>max, natural/in-range/below/above cases,
wrapped and alignment, TextButton/TextBox, non-scaled labels, add/change/
remove/re-add ordering, pre-add TextScaled ordering, nested AutoXY, clamped
non-scaled auto content, and the wrap coupling.  Native 2.743 produced 116
identical lines on three fresh processes and Boblox produced the same 116
lines; committed outputs are
`tests/live/output/automatic_size_{native,boblox}.txt` with the three raw
native samples in
`tests/live/output/automatic_size_uitextsizeconstraint_native_samples.txt`.
`ui_constraints_probe.lua` (25 rows) and `gui_layout_probe.lua` (103 rows)
also remain byte-identical.  `unit_gui` now has a constraint case for the
defaults, write clamp, min>max, zero, fixed/wrapped/non-scaled, remove/re-add,
alignment defaults, nested AutoXY and clamped non-scaled auto content; the
full binary is 28 cases / 1,835 assertions, all passing.

## Legacy text metrics

The former em-table approximation is gone.  The native client's five Legacy
faces are shaped with FreeType hinted advances and HarfBuzz GPOS kerning at
fractional pixel sizes, so the exact quantized results depend on the native
FreeType build.  Rather than approximate them, `font_metrics_probe.lua`
captured ASCII and `font_metrics_capture.lua` captured a 727-codepoint
charset at every integer TextSize 1..100 for all five faces.  The charset
covers Latin-1, Latin Extended-A, combining marks, general punctuation,
currency, letterlike symbols, arrows, mathematical operators, box drawing,
block/geometric shapes, common UI symbols and U+FFFD.  A codepoint outside
the set uses the captured per-face/size U+FFFD replacement advance.  The sizes
clamp to `[1, 100]` and then floor before lookup; `TextBounds.Y` is the
floored line height.  The tables live in
`rbx_runtime/src/gui/gui_font_metrics_data.inc` and are consumed by one module,
`rbx_runtime/src/gui/gui_font_metrics.cpp`.

Adjacent non-ASCII GPOS adjustments are not inferred from FreeType or
HarfBuzz.  `font_metrics_pairs_probe.lua` measures eight native pair targets
(`Vé`, `fé`, `o”`, `rö`, `rø`, `s”`, `é?`, `Łó`) at every size on SourceSans
and SourceSansBold; the generator derives each adjustment from the captured
pair width minus the two captured standalone advances.  The ASCII pair
tables remain native-exact (773 SourceSans, 762 SourceSansBold pairs).  A
pair outside those tables contributes zero; no guessed kerning is applied.
Contextual shaping that spans more than one adjacent pair is likewise not
synthesized.  The one observed exception is the `! ¿Q` context, which stays
3 px narrower on native than the additive model at some large sizes; it was
recorded with a direct native probe and is not special-cased.  Combining-mark
advances are exact, while their painted mark offsets still come from the
renderer's FreeType shaping.

For a line, integer glyph advances and adjacent GPOS adjustments are additive;
adjustments around spaces are captured in context, and leading spaces carry no
kerning until the first non-space glyph.  Widths round once at the end of the
line.  Line height is `floor(1.5 * TextSize)` for Legacy and `floor(TextSize)`
for Arial, ArialBold, SourceSans and SourceSansBold.  Newline height is lines
times that height.  Fractional TextScaled/UIScale sizes use the nearest
captured integer for the glyph advance while still reporting the fractional
line height, matching the native 100x20 `Hello` fit (`41x20`, not `42x20`).

TextBounds, layout, wrapping, TextFits, AutomaticSize, the TextBox caret and
the renderer's caret/selection all call this module.  The renderer no longer
installs a NanoVG measurer, so no presentation path can disagree with the
runtime.  The renderer loads Arimo-Regular/Bold and SourceSansPro-Regular/Bold
and draws each face at its native pixel scale (1.3125 for Legacy, 0.875 for
Arial, 0.8 for SourceSans).

`tests/live/font_metrics_probe.lua` covers every face at sizes 1, 8 and
9..100 with eight strings, a wrapped long line, TextFits and AutomaticSize.XY.
`tests/live/font_metrics_extended_probe.lua` adds 18 non-ASCII/fractional
strings at sizes 0.25, 1.99, 13.01, 13.99, 50.50, 99.99, 100, 100.01, 150
and 1000 on all five faces, including AutomaticSize; its 50 data rows plus
`DONE` are byte-identical at
`tests/live/output/font_metrics_extended_native.txt`.

A fixed wrapped label clips `TextBounds.Y` to the authored height while
`TextFits` describes the whole string: native SourceSans 50 renders the probe
line as `74x300` in an 80x300 label and `74x350` in an 80x400 label.  That
clip was re-captured directly from native for this pass.  A word wider than
the box is broken at characters, matching native.  The committed
`tests/live/output/font_metrics_native.txt` and `font_metrics_boblox.txt` are
byte-identical across repeated native runs and Boblox.  The original
AutomaticSize probe remains 116-byte-identical lines, and
`ui_constraints_probe.lua` (25 rows) and `gui_layout_probe.lua` (103 rows)
still diff empty.  `unit_gui` pins `Hello` at 10 px and every 67..100 px size
for all five faces, plus `AVATAR`/`Hello world` and line heights at 100 px;
the non-ASCII/fractional cases, wrapped clip/TextFits, AutomaticSize and caret
prefix geometry are covered by the two added cases (28 cases, 1,835
assertions).

### `0xA1` attribute replication and null-ref boundaries (2026-11-15)

The 189707 0x97 tail is the sparse writer-slot variant.  The tail is
`[u32 entry_count][entry...][8-byte trailer]`, where each entry is
`[u32 class_id][u32 count][count * u32 global_member_id]` and only the 18
classes with slots appear; the 1818 form is the dense variant whose entry
count equals the schema class count and whose first class id is zero.  Both
end in the same trailer.  `parseJoinSchema` now accepts both and requires an
exact trailer.  With the sparse tail, the 189707 class 366 slot list is
`5664/5665/5668` -> members `PhysicsRepRootPart`, `PhysicsRepRootRef`,
`ReplicationPV`, so mask bit 2 is tag 57 rather than a fallback member.

The 189707 body container is the same as 1818; the apparent mismatch was a
misread of the u16 after the mask.  It is not a cap on a value: `sub_6431154`
captures `v18 = stream_pos + u16` after reading the dirty set, and every
early-out restores that end.  The serializer patches the same span with
`v125 - v143 - 2` after writing every selected value and attribute.  Native
readers therefore read a tag from the shared stream without a per-body limit
and only then seek to the recorded end.

`parseAppStateBody` now models that contract.  `AppStateRecord::lookahead`
carries the contiguous frame tail after the body, so a bit-packed value can
consume its guard bytes; a body that ends past the value reports the skipped
bytes and restores the end; a final record whose captured frame stops exactly
on the body gets the same zeroed stream guard native's buffer provides.

The skip is now accounted per body instead of being an unmeasured remainder.
Every body byte lands in exactly one of `decoded_bytes`, `end_seek_skipped` or
`unaccounted`; the parser hard-fails if the decoded span is short of the reader
position, if an empty mask is paired with a non-empty body, or if more than
eight bytes remain after the last selected value.  Full 189707 result:
`total=105817 decoded=94301 end_skipped=11516 unaccounted=0`, with 5,781
lookahead guard bytes outside the recorded bodies; 2,755 bodies carry the
11,516 skipped bytes, maximum 8.  Every one of those 2,755 records has
`mask_words == {4}`: only `Part.ReplicationPV` (tag 57) is selected, there are
no attribute bits, and no selected member remains after the PV reader returns.
The 1818 corpus has `total=545049 decoded=545049 end_skipped=0 unaccounted=0`
over its closed bodies (9,017 tag-57 values); its 213 pre-capture attribute
values, whose schemas were established before the capture window, remain a
separate 8,094-byte partial category rather than being counted as padding.

The proof that those 11,516 bytes are not an undecoded member is the native
reader itself.  `sub_6431154` walks only the dirty-set bits and calls
`sub_6432F94` once per selected bit; for bit 2 that case is `sub_6427536`,
which reads the flags byte, three exponent nibbles, the selected component
blocks and the orientation selector, then returns.  There is no fall-through
read after the orientation switch.  The body end is captured as `v142` before
the loop and restored by the `LABEL_218` path, so the tail is not revisited by
the named-value reader.  With no attribute bits set, it is not an attribute
value either.  The tails are nonzero and their body lengths cluster at fixed
capacity sizes (24, 17, 14 and 19 bytes) while the PV consumes different
widths, so they are server-side buffer slack, not zero alignment and not a
fixed field that the native reader exposes.

`unit_replicate` pins the accounting (decoded/end-skip/guard/unaccounted), an
empty-mask body with orphan bytes, and a valid PV followed by sixteen junk
bytes; both malformed cases must fail instead of being absorbed as padding.

The attribute section of a server `0xA1` record was the last unmodelled part of
the per-instance state stream.  IDA's `InstanceStateSerialization` deserializer
(`sub_6431154`, lines ~543–636) shows the mask has a named-writer region and
then paired attribute entries: `named + 2*i` is a schema entry
`00 <tag> <u32 name length> <name>`, and `named + 2*i + 1` is the value in the
ordinary tagged-value grammar.  A schema can arrive without its value, and a
value can arrive in a later record, so the codec stores the schema per entity
(`A1AttributeSchemas`) and applies values as they arrive.  Part records name 56
writer slots (bits 56/57 are the first schema/value pair), Tool records name 15
slots (bit 14 is named; 15/16 and 17/18 are two pairs with member count 19), and
Humanoid records use 48/49/50 for the same layout.

The live 1818 corpus (`/tmp/a1dump`, 2,000 frames, `/tmp/a1schema.txt`) now
closes every frame: `frames_ok=2000 frame_fail=0 records=27666 closed=27666
tailed=0 attrs=452 nullrecs=4447`.  The 4,447 null-ref records are decoded as
null records now; the old boundary rule silently aliased that many heads.  The 189707 capture frames 333/333 and records 5,508/5,508 with no null refs;
its header flags are `0x44000001` (client-authority mode), and its bodies use
the same named-value/attribute grammar once the end-seek contract above is
applied.  Attribute value tags in the 1818 corpus are string `2` ×415,
bool `9` ×1, Vector2 `21` ×13 and Vector3 `22` ×23.

`Part.ReplicationPV` (schema tag 57) is no longer opaque.  The live build's
alternate branch in `sub_6432F94` case `0x39` is `sub_6427536`: a flags byte
(bit0 position absent, bits1–2 high position exponent, bits3–4 orientation
kind, bit5 linear velocity absent, bit6 angular velocity absent), a bit-packed
position block and two optional bit-packed velocity blocks, then the
orientation table selector (`sub_6427317`: kind 0 none, 1 u32, 2 u32+u8,
3 u32+u16).  Widths are `ceil(exp + 6.643856189774724)` = 12/20/28/36/44/52
bits and components are signed fixed-point with native dequantization
`raw * 2^(exp - bits + 1)` (`sub_5E7D9AE`).  `repl_pv.{h,cpp}` implements that
exact codec; `unit_replicate` pins the three live 24/14/9-byte vectors and the
"followed by another member" extent.  The value is Hidden, NotScriptable and
RobloxSecurity, so Boblox keeps it in engine state (`ApplyReport::replication_pv`)
and does not expose it through Luau, matching native.

One corpus caveat is now explicit: 213 of the 27,666 values are attribute
values whose schema arrived before the captured window (for example a Part
attribute `f64` whose name/type is absent).  They stay partial and are never
guessed from their bytes; this is a capture-boundary schema gap, not a tag-57
failure.  The enum-schema form (`00 07 <u32 enum len> <enum name> <u32 name
len> <name>`) was added after the live 1818 replay exposed an extra name that
the first pass skipped; `HumanoidStateType` / `previousHumanoidState` is pinned
by a unit case.

The native 1818 probe saw 5,664 descendants, 100 instances with attributes and
206 attribute rows (16 stable `RBX*` rows).  The delayed Boblox probe saw the
same 11 stable `RBX*` rows on shared instances, byte/value-identical to native;
the five native-only rows belonged to accessory instances Boblox had not spawned,
not to a decoder difference.  `/tmp/a1_net_native2.log` and
`/tmp/a1_net_bob2.log` are the comparison inputs.

A null ref is a one-byte record ref with no u32 id, so its head is four bytes
shorter.  The framing walk now tries that layout only at the exact end of the
previous body, never at every scan offset, and requires the candidate body to
decode against a copy of the live attribute-schema map.  This is the
body-decodability disambiguation that the earlier 2,012-frame experiment lacked:
the old corpus measured 1,664 null boundaries and 12 lost frames with the
five-byte-only rule, while permissive null acceptance lost 25.  A synthetic
unit test pins a normal record, a marker-prefixed null record and an adjacent
null record; the live 1818 corpus supplies 4,447 real null boundaries.  A
zero-length null body is deliberately not accepted as a boundary candidate
because it is indistinguishable from a shifted read; no such record appeared in
the live dumps.

Replicated attribute writes now queue `AttributeChanged` on the instance and the
stream layer drains it with the Luau state after that record is applied,
matching the runtime's existing local `SetAttribute` signal semantics.
